As mentioned above, we will separate the analysis of the low-degree part of the feature matrix
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}
and the high-degree part
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}
. For the high-degree part, we will simply use the concentration stated in Lemma
B.3
. For the low degree part, we will study functional of
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}
with regularization
\gamma_{+}=p\nu_{1}+{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})
. We therefore introduce an effective regularization
\nu_{2,0}
associated to the model
(p,{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0},\gamma_{+})
, i.e., the unique positive solution to the equation

p-\frac{\gamma_{+}}{\nu_{2,0}}={\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2,0})^{-1}\big).

(80)

Intuitively,
\nu_{2,0}
will be closed to
\nu_{2}
as soon as
\lambda_{\max}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})\ll\nu_{2}
as

n-\frac{p\nu_{1}}{\nu_{2}}\approx{\rm Tr}\left({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2})^{-1}\right)+\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})}{\nu_{2}},

and uniqueness of the positive solution
\nu_{2,0}
. This is formalized in the following lemma.

Lemma B.5

(Truncated fixed point)
.

Let
{\mathsf{m}}
be chosen such that
p^{2}\xi_{{\mathsf{m}}+1}^{2}\leq\gamma_{\lambda}
, then

\frac{|\nu_{2,0}-\nu_{2}|}{\nu_{2}}\leq\frac{1}{p}.

(81)

Furthermore, there exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that

\left|{\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2,0})^{-1}\big)+\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})}{\nu_{2,0}}-{\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-1}\big)\right|\leq\frac{C}{p}{\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-1}\big).

(82)

Proof of Lemma
B.5
.

The first bound (
81
) follows directly from
[
Misiakiewicz and Saeed, 2024
, Lemma 6]
. For the second inequality, we decompose this difference into

\displaystyle\left|{\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2,0})^{-1}\big)+\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})}{\nu_{2,0}}-{\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-1}\big)\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left|\nu_{2,0}-\nu_{2}\right|{\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2,0})^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2})^{-1}\big)+\frac{\xi_{{\mathsf{m}}+1}^{2}+|\nu_{2,0}-\nu_{2}|}{\nu_{2,0}}{\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+}+\nu_{2})^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{\xi_{{\mathsf{m}}+1}^{2}}{\nu_{2,0}}+\frac{|\nu_{2,0}-\nu_{2}|}{\nu_{2,0}}\right\}{\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{3}{p}{\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-1}\big),

where we used that
\xi_{{\mathsf{m}}+1}^{2}/\nu_{2,0}\leq\gamma_{+}/(p^{2}\nu_{2,0})\leq 1/p
by the assumption on
{\mathsf{m}}
and identity (
80
).
∎

B.3
Deterministic equivalents for functionals of
\mathbb{Z}
conditional on
\mathbb{F}

As mentioned in Section
B.1
, we will first show that the test error concentrates over
{\boldsymbol{Z}}
conditional on
{\boldsymbol{F}}
on some quantity that only depends on
\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{{\boldsymbol{F}}}
. The bias and variance terms can be written in terms of the following three functionals of the feature matrix
{\boldsymbol{Z}}
: for a general p.s.d. matrix
{\boldsymbol{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
and positive scalar
\kappa>0
, define

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}({\boldsymbol{Z}};\kappa):=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle Tr}\left(\frac{{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}}{n}({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}+\kappa)^{-1}\right),

(83)

\displaystyle\Phi_{3}({\boldsymbol{Z}};{\boldsymbol{A}},\kappa):=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle Tr}\left({\boldsymbol{A}}\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}^{1/2}({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}+\kappa)^{-1}\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}+\kappa)^{-1}\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}^{1/2}\right),

\displaystyle\Phi_{4}({\boldsymbol{Z}};{\boldsymbol{A}},\kappa):=

\displaystyle\left({\boldsymbol{A}}\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}^{1/2}({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}+\kappa)^{-1}\frac{{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}}{n}({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}+\kappa)^{-1}\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}^{1/2}\right),

where we recall that
{\boldsymbol{Z}}
has i.i.d. rows with covariance
\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}={\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}/p
. We show that these functional are well approximated by functionals of
{\boldsymbol{F}}
that can be written in terms of the following functionals:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};\kappa):=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle Tr}\left(\frac{{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}}{p}({\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}+\kappa)^{-1}\right),

(84)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}},\kappa):=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\cdot\frac{\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}},\kappa)}{n-\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},\kappa)},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}},\kappa):=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle Tr}\left({\boldsymbol{A}}({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}})^{2}({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}+\kappa)^{-2}\right).

The following proposition gather the approximation guarantees for
\Phi_{2},\Phi_{3},\Phi_{4}
listed in Eq. (
83
).

Proposition B.6

(Deterministic equivalents for
\Phi({\boldsymbol{Z}})
conditional on
{\boldsymbol{F}}
)
.

Under Assumption
B.1
and assuming that
{\boldsymbol{F}}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{F}}
defined in Eq. (
79
), for any
D,K>0
, there exist constants
\eta_{*}\in(0,1/4)
,
C_{D,K}>0
, and
C_{*,D,K}>0
such that the followings holds. Let
\rho_{\kappa}(p)
and
\widetilde{\rho}_{\kappa}(n,p)
be defined as per Eqs. (
26
) and (
25
). For any
n\geq C_{D,K}
and regularization parameter
\lambda>0
, if it holds that

\displaystyle\lambda\geq n^{-K},\qquad\qquad\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)^{5/2}\log^{3/2}(n)\leq K\sqrt{n},

(85)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)^{2}\cdot\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)^{5/2}\log^{3}(p)\leq K\sqrt{p},

then for any p.s.d. matrix
{\boldsymbol{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
(independent of
{\boldsymbol{Z}}|{\boldsymbol{F}}
), with probability at least
1-n^{-D}
on
{\boldsymbol{Z}}
conditional on
{\boldsymbol{F}}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\Phi_{2}({\boldsymbol{Z}};\lambda)-\frac{p}{n}\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};p\nu_{1})\right|\leq

\displaystyle~C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(n,p)\cdot\frac{p}{n}\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};p\nu_{1}),

(86)

\displaystyle\left|\Phi_{3}({\boldsymbol{Z}};{\boldsymbol{A}},\lambda)-\left(\frac{n\nu_{1}}{\lambda}\right)^{2}\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}},p\nu_{1})\right|\leq

\displaystyle~C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(n,p)\cdot\left(\frac{n\nu_{1}}{\lambda}\right)^{2}\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}},p\nu_{1}),

(87)

\displaystyle\left|\Phi_{4}({\boldsymbol{Z}};{\boldsymbol{A}},\lambda)-\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}},p\nu_{1})\right|\leq

\displaystyle~C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(n,p)\cdot\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}},p\nu_{1}),

(88)

where the approximation rate is given by

{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(n,p):=\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)^{6}\log^{5/2}(n)}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)^{2}\cdot\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)^{5/2}\log^{3}(p)}{\sqrt{p}}.

(89)

Proposition
B.6
is a consequence of
[
Misiakiewicz and Saeed, 2024
, Theorem 4]
(see Appendix
A
and Theorem
A.2
for background) and Proposition
B.4
. We defer its proof to Section
B.7.3
. Note that the term
\widetilde{O}(p^{-1/2})
in the approximation rate
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(n,p)
defined in Eq. (
89
) comes from comparing
p\nu_{1}
with
p\tilde{\nu}_{1}
and is equal to
\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(n,p)
where
{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(n,p)
is defined in Proposition
B.4
. If instead, we compared to functionals with regularization
p\tilde{\nu}_{1}
, then the approximation rate in Proposition
B.6
would scale
\widetilde{O}(n^{-1/2})
as expected.

In the analysis of the bias term, we will further need to show deterministic equivalents in the case where
{\boldsymbol{A}}
is itself a random matrix uncorrelated (but not independent) to
{\boldsymbol{Z}}|{\boldsymbol{F}}
. The following proposition gather these approximation guarantees and is a consequence of
[
Misiakiewicz and Saeed, 2024
, Lemma 10]
and Proposition
B.4
.

Proposition B.7

(Deterministic equivalents for
\Phi({\boldsymbol{Z}})
, uncorrelated numerator)
.

Assume the same setting as Proposition
B.6
and the same conditions (
85
). Consider a deterministic vector
{\boldsymbol{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and a random vector
{\boldsymbol{u}}=(u_{i})_{i\in[n]}
with i.i.d. entries and
\mathbb{E}[u_{i}]=0
,
\mathbb{E}[u_{i}^{2}]=1
, and
\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}u_{i}|{\boldsymbol{F}}]={\boldsymbol{0}}
. Then with probability at least
1-n^{-D}
on
{\boldsymbol{Z}}
conditional on
{\boldsymbol{F}}
, we have

\displaystyle~\left|\langle{\boldsymbol{u}},{\boldsymbol{Z}}({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}+\lambda)^{-1}\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}+\lambda)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{u}}\rangle-n\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})\right|\leq C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(n,p),

(90)

\displaystyle~\left|\langle{\boldsymbol{u}},{\boldsymbol{Z}}({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}+\lambda)^{-1}\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}+\lambda)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{v}}\rangle\right|\leq C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(n,p)\cdot\frac{n\nu_{1}}{\lambda}\sqrt{\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};\overline{{\boldsymbol{v}}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{v}}}^{\mathsf{T}},p\nu_{1})},

(91)

where we denoted
\overline{{\boldsymbol{v}}}:=\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{v}}
and the approximation rate is given by

{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(n,p):=\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)^{6}\log^{7/2}(n)}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)^{2}\cdot\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)^{5/2}\log^{3}(p)}{\sqrt{p}}.

(92)

We defer the proof of Proposition
B.7
to Section
B.7.3
.

B.4
Deterministic equivalents for functionals of
\mathbb{F}

After replacing the bias and variance terms by their deterministic equivalents over the randomness in
{\boldsymbol{Z}}|{\boldsymbol{F}}
, we obtain functionals in terms of
\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};p\nu_{1})
and
\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}},p\nu_{1})
listed in Eq. (
84
). As mentioned in Section
B.2
, we will analyze the low-degree and high degree part of the feature matrix separately. Using Lemma
B.3
, we can replace the high-degree part
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}^{\mathsf{T}}
by a deterministic matrix, which results in a regularization parameter
\gamma(p\nu_{1})=p\nu_{1}+{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})
.

The functionals of
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}
can be written in terms of the following quantities: for any deterministic matrix
{\boldsymbol{B}}\in\mathbb{R}^{{\mathsf{m}}\times{\mathsf{m}}}
, define

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{B}},\kappa)=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle Tr}\left({\boldsymbol{B}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{1/2}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}+\gamma(\kappa))^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{1/2}\right),

(93)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};\kappa)=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle Tr}\left(\frac{{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}}{p}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}+\gamma(\kappa))^{-1}\right),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{3}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{B}},\kappa)=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle Tr}\left({\boldsymbol{B}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}_{0}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}+\gamma(\kappa))^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}+\gamma(\kappa))^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{1/2}\right),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{4}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{B}},\kappa)=

\displaystyle\left({\boldsymbol{B}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{1/2}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}+\gamma(\kappa))^{-1}\frac{{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}}{p}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}+\gamma(\kappa))^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\right).

We show that these functionals can be well approximated by the deterministic functions that can be written in terms of

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}(\nu;{\boldsymbol{B}})=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle Tr}\left({\boldsymbol{B}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu)^{-1}\right),

(94)

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}(\nu)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}\left({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu)^{-1}\right),

\displaystyle\Psi_{3}(\nu;{\boldsymbol{B}})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\cdot\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{B}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu)^{-2})}{p-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu)^{-2})}.

Recall that for
\kappa=p\nu_{1}
, we denote
\gamma_{+}:=\gamma(p\nu_{1})
and
\nu_{2,0}
the effective regularization associated to model
(p,{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0},\gamma_{+})
. The following proposition gather the approximation guarantees for
\widetilde{\Phi}_{1},\widetilde{\Phi}_{2},\widetilde{\Phi}_{3},\widetilde{\Phi}_{4}
listed in Eq. (
93
).

Proposition B.8

(Deterministic equivalents for
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}
)
.

Under Assumption
B.1
, for any
D,K>0
, there exist constants
\eta_{*}\in(0,1/4)
,
C_{D,K}>0
, and
C_{*,D,K}>0
such that the followings holds. Let
\rho_{\kappa}(p)
be defined as per Eq. (
26
). For any
p\geq C_{D,K}
and
\lambda>0
, if it holds that

\displaystyle\gamma_{+}\geq p^{-K},\qquad\qquad\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)^{5/2}\log^{3/2}(p)\leq K\sqrt{p},

(95)

then for any deterministic p.s.d. matrix
{\boldsymbol{B}}\in\mathbb{R}^{{\mathsf{m}}\times{\mathsf{m}}}
, with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{B}},p\nu_{1})-\frac{\nu_{2,0}}{\gamma_{+}}\Psi_{1}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{B}})\right|\leq

\displaystyle~C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\cdot\frac{\nu_{2,0}}{\gamma_{+}}\Psi_{1}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{B}}),

(96)

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};p\nu_{1})-\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0})\right|\leq

\displaystyle~C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\cdot\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0}),

(97)

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\Phi}_{3}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{B}},p\nu_{1})-\left(\frac{p\nu_{2,0}}{\gamma_{+}}\right)^{2}\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{B}})\right|\leq

\displaystyle~C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\cdot\left(\frac{p\nu_{2,0}}{\gamma_{+}}\right)^{2}\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{B}}),

(98)

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\Phi}_{4}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{B}},p\nu_{1})-\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{B}})\right|\leq

\displaystyle~C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\cdot\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{B}}),

(99)

where the approximation rate is given by

{\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p):=\frac{\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)^{6}\log^{3}(p)}{\sqrt{p}}.

(100)

This proposition is obtained by directly applying Theorem
A.2
with no modifications.

Again, in the analysis of the bias term, because we separated the analysis of the low-degree and high-degree parts
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}
and
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}
, we will further need deterministic equivalents when
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is itself a random matrix uncorrelated but not independent to
{\boldsymbol{F}}
. We gather the associated deterministic equivalents in the following proposition.

Proposition B.9

(Deterministic equivalents for
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}
, uncorrelated numerator)
.

Assume the same setting as Proposition
B.8
and the same conditions (
95
). Consider a deterministic vector
{\boldsymbol{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}}
and a random vector
{\boldsymbol{u}}=(u_{j})_{j\in[p]}
with i.i.d. entries and
\mathbb{E}[u_{j}]=0
,
\mathbb{E}[u_{j}^{2}]=1
, and
\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{f}_{0,j}u_{j}]={\boldsymbol{0}}
. Then with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
, we have

\displaystyle~\left|\frac{1}{p}\langle{\boldsymbol{u}},({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}+\gamma_{+})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{u}}\rangle-\frac{1}{(p\nu_{2,0})^{2}}\frac{1}{1-\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2,0})^{-2})}\right|\leq C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{4}(p)\cdot\frac{1}{\gamma_{+}^{2}},

(101)

\displaystyle~\left|\frac{1}{p}\langle{\boldsymbol{u}},({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}+\gamma_{+})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{v}}\rangle\right|\leq C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{4}(p)\frac{p\nu_{2,0}}{\gamma_{+}^{2}}\sqrt{\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2,0};\overline{{\boldsymbol{v}}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{v}}}^{\mathsf{T}})},

(102)

where we denoted
\overline{{\boldsymbol{v}}}:={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{v}}
and the approximation rate is given by

{\mathcal{E}}_{4}(p):=\frac{\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)^{6}\log^{7/2}(p)}{\sqrt{p}}.

(103)

This proposition is a direct consequence of
[
Misiakiewicz and Saeed, 2024
, Lemma 10]
.

Throughout the proofs, with a slight abuse of notations, we will denote functionals
\widetilde{\Phi}_{i}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{B}},\kappa)
,
i\in[4]
, the functionals listed in Eq. (
93
) applied to the truncated feature matrix
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}
, with covariance
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}
, regularization
\gamma(\kappa)
, and deterministic matrix
{\boldsymbol{B}}\in\mathbb{R}^{{\mathsf{m}}\times{\mathsf{m}}}
, and
\widetilde{\Phi}_{i}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{B}},\kappa)
,
i\in[4]
, the functionals applied to the full feature matrix
{\boldsymbol{F}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times\infty}
, where
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}
is replaced by
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
, the regularization parameter is
\kappa
, and the deterministic matrix
{\boldsymbol{B}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty\times\infty}
. Similarly, we will us the notation
\Psi_{i}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{B}}),i\in[3]
for the truncated deterministic functionals (
94
), and the notation
\Psi_{i}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{B}}),i\in[3]
to denote the full functionals with
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}
replaced by
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
and
{\boldsymbol{B}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty\times\infty}
.

B.5
Approximation guarantee for the variance term

Recall the expressions for the variance term

{\mathcal{V}}({\boldsymbol{G}},{\boldsymbol{F}},\lambda)=\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\cdot{\rm Tr}\big(\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}+\lambda)^{-2}\big),

and its associated deterministic equivalent

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}_{n,p}(\lambda)=

\displaystyle~\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\frac{\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}{1-\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})},

(104)

\displaystyle\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})=

\displaystyle~\frac{p}{n}\left[\left(1-\frac{\nu_{1}}{\nu_{2}}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{\nu_{1}}{\nu_{2}}\right)^{2}\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})}{p-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})}\right].

(105)

We prove in this section an approximation guarantee between
{\mathcal{V}}({\boldsymbol{G}},{\boldsymbol{F}},\lambda)
and
\mathsf{V}_{n,p}(\lambda)
. For convenience, we state a separate theorem for this term.

Theorem B.10

(Deterministic equivalent for the variance term)
.

Assume the features
({\boldsymbol{z}}_{i})_{i\in[n]}
and
(\boldsymbol{f}_{j})_{j\in[p]}
satisfy Assumption
B.1
, and the covariance
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
and target coefficients
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}
satisfy Assumption
3.2
. Then, for any
D,K>0
, there exist constants
\eta_{*}\in(0,1/2)
and
C_{*,D,K}>0
such that the following holds. Let
\rho_{\kappa}
and
\widetilde{\rho}_{\kappa}
be defined as per Eqs. (
26
) and (
25
). For any
n,p\geq C_{*,D,K}
and
\lambda>0
, if it holds that

\displaystyle\lambda\geq n^{-K},\qquad\gamma_{\lambda}\geq p^{-K},\qquad\qquad\quad\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)^{5/2}\cdot\log^{3/2}(n)\leq

\displaystyle K\sqrt{n},

(106)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)^{2}\cdot\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)^{7}\cdot\log^{4}(p)\leq

\displaystyle K\sqrt{p},

then with probability at least
1-n^{-D}-p^{-D}
, we have

\left|{\mathcal{V}}({\boldsymbol{G}},{\boldsymbol{F}},\lambda)-\mathsf{V}_{n,p}(\lambda)\right|\leq C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{V}(n,p)\cdot\mathsf{V}_{n,p}(\lambda),

where the approximation rate is given by

{\mathcal{E}}_{V}(n,p):=\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)^{6}\log^{5/2}(n)}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)^{2}\cdot\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)^{7}\log^{3}(p)}{\sqrt{p}}.

(107)

Proof of Theorem
B.10
.

First, note that
{\mathcal{V}}({\boldsymbol{G}},{\boldsymbol{F}},\lambda)
can be written in terms of the functional
\Phi_{4}
defined in Eq. (
83
):

{\mathcal{V}}({\boldsymbol{G}},{\boldsymbol{F}},\lambda)=\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\cdot n\Phi_{4}({\boldsymbol{Z}};{\mathbf{I}},\lambda).

Recall that
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{F}}
is the event defined in Eq. (
79
). Under the assumptions of Theorem
B.10
, we can apply Lemma
B.2
and Proposition
B.4
to obtain

\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{F}})\geq 1-p^{-D}.

Hence, applying Proposition
B.6
for
{\boldsymbol{F}}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{F}}
and via union bound, we obtain that with probability at least
1-p^{-D}-n^{-D}
,

\left|n\Phi_{4}({\boldsymbol{Z}};{\mathbf{I}},\lambda)-n\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})\right|\leq C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(p,n)\cdot n\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1}),

(108)

where
{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(n,p)
is defined in Eq. (
89
) and we recall the expressions

n\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})=\frac{\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})}{n-\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})},\qquad\quad\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})={\rm Tr}\big(({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}})^{2}({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}+p\nu_{1})^{-2}\big).

Let us decompose
\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})
into

\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})={\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}+p\nu_{1})^{-1})-p\nu_{1}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}+p\nu_{1})^{-2}).

From Lemma
B.11
stated below, we have with probability at least
1-p^{-D}

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}+p\nu_{1})^{-1})-\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2})\right|\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\cdot\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}),

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}+p\nu_{1})^{-2})-\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1})\right|\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\cdot\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p){\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\cdot\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1}),

where
{\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)
is the approximation rate defined in Eq. (
100
).
Note that

\displaystyle p\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2})-p^{2}\nu_{1}\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1})=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-1})-\frac{\nu_{1}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})}{1-\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p\left\{1-\frac{\nu_{1}}{\nu_{2}}-\frac{\nu_{1}}{\nu_{2}}\frac{1-\frac{\nu_{1}}{\nu_{2}}-\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})}{1-\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p\left\{\left(1-\frac{\nu_{1}}{\nu_{2}}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{\nu_{1}}{\nu_{2}}\right)^{2}\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})}{p-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2}).

Combining the above displays, we deduce that

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})-n\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})\right|\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\cdot\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p){\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\cdot\left[p\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2})+p^{2}\nu_{1}\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1})\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\cdot\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p){\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\cdot\left[n\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})+2p^{2}\nu_{1}\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1})\right]

Using Eq. (
120
) in Lemma
B.14
stated in Section
B.7
, we conclude that with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
,

\left|\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})-n\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})\right|\leq C_{*,D,K}\cdot\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p){\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\cdot n\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2}).

(109)

Finally, by simple algebra, we have

\displaystyle\left|n\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})-\frac{\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}{1-\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}\right|

(110)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left\{n\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})+1\right\}\frac{|\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})-n\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})|}{n-\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left\{\left|n\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})-\frac{\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}{1-\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}\right|+\frac{1}{1-\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}\right\}\cdot C_{*,D,K}\cdot\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p){\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\frac{\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}{1-\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}.

Note that by Eq. (
119
) in Lemma
B.14
, we have

\frac{\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}{1-\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}\leq(1-\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2}))^{-1}\leq C_{*}\cdot\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p).

Hence rearranging the terms in Eq. (
110
) and using from conditions (
106
) and that
p\geq C_{*,D,K}
, we obtain with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
that

\left|n\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})-\frac{\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}{1-\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}\right|\leq C_{*,D,K}\cdot\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p){\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\frac{\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}{1-\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}.

(111)

Using an union bound and combining bounds Eqs. (
108
) and (
111
), we obtain with probability at least
1-n^{-D}-p^{-D}
, that

\displaystyle\left|{\mathcal{V}}({\boldsymbol{G}},{\boldsymbol{F}},\lambda)-\mathsf{V}_{n,p}(\lambda)\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left|{\mathcal{V}}({\boldsymbol{G}},{\boldsymbol{F}},\lambda)-\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\cdot n\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})\right|+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\left|n\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})-\frac{\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}{1-\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\cdot\left\{{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(p,n)+\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p){\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\right\}\cdot\left[\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\cdot n\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})+\mathsf{V}_{n,p}(\lambda)\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\cdot\left\{{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(p,n)+\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p){\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\right\}\cdot\mathsf{V}_{n,p}(\lambda),

where we used Eq. (
111
) and conditions (
106
) in the last line. Replacing the rates
{\mathcal{E}}_{j}
by their expressions conclude the proof of this theorem.
∎

Lemma B.11
.

Under the setting of Theorem
B.10
and assuming the same conditions (
106
), we have with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}+p\nu_{1})^{-1})-\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2})\right|\leq

\displaystyle~C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\cdot\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}),

(112)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}+p\nu_{1})^{-2})-\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1})\right|\leq

\displaystyle~C_{*,D,K}\cdot\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p){\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\cdot\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1}),

(113)

where
{\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)
is the approximation rate defined in Eq. (
100
).

The proof of this lemma can be found in Section
B.7.4
.

B.6
Approximation guarantee for the bias term

Recall the expression for the bias term

\mathcal{B}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*};{\boldsymbol{G}},{\boldsymbol{F}},\lambda)=\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}-p^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}+\lambda)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{G}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\|_{2}^{2},

and its associated deterministic equivalent

\displaystyle\chi(\nu_{2})=

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})}{p-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})},

\displaystyle\mathsf{B}_{n,p}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},\lambda)=

\displaystyle\frac{\nu_{2}^{2}}{1-\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}\Big[\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle+\chi(\nu_{2})\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle\Big].

We prove in this section an approximation guarantee between
\mathcal{B}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*};{\boldsymbol{G}},{\boldsymbol{F}},\lambda)
and
\mathsf{B}_{n,p}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},\lambda)
. For convenience, we state a separate theorem for this term.

Theorem B.12

(Deterministic equivalent for the bias term)
.

Assume the features
({\boldsymbol{z}}_{i})_{i\in[n]}
and
(\boldsymbol{f}_{j})_{j\in[p]}
satisfy Assumption
B.1
, and that there exists
{\mathsf{m}}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
p^{2}\xi^{2}_{{\mathsf{m}}}\leq\gamma_{{\mathsf{m}}}(n\lambda/p)
. Then, for any
D,K>0
, there exist constants
\eta_{*}\in(0,1/2)
and
C_{*,D,K}>0
such that the following holds. Let
\rho_{\kappa}
and
\widetilde{\rho}_{\kappa}
be defined as per Eqs. (
26
) and (
25
), and recall that
\gamma_{\lambda}:=\gamma_{\mathsf{m}}(n\lambda/p)
and
\gamma_{+}:=\gamma_{\mathsf{m}}(p\nu_{1})
. For any
n,p\geq C_{*,D,K}
and
\lambda>0
, if it holds that

\displaystyle\lambda\geq n^{-K},\qquad\gamma_{\lambda}\geq p^{-K},\qquad\qquad\quad\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)^{5/2}\cdot\log^{3/2}(n)\leq

\displaystyle K\sqrt{n},

(114)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)^{2}\cdot\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)^{8}\cdot\log^{4}(p)\leq

\displaystyle K\sqrt{p},

then with probability at least
1-n^{-D}-p^{-D}
, we have

\left|\mathcal{B}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*};{\boldsymbol{G}},{\boldsymbol{F}},\lambda)-\mathsf{B}_{n,p}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},\lambda)\right|\leq C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{B}(n,p)\cdot\mathsf{B}_{n,p}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},\lambda),

where the approximation rate is given by

{\mathcal{E}}_{B}(n,p):=\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)^{6}\log^{7/2}(n)}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)^{2}\cdot\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)^{8}\log^{7/2}(p)}{\sqrt{p}}.

(115)

Before starting the proof, let us introduce some notations. First, define
{\mathsf{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{F}}}
the projection onto the span of
{\boldsymbol{F}}
, and
{\mathsf{P}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}}
the projection orthogonal to the span of
{\boldsymbol{F}}
, i.e.,

{\mathsf{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{F}}}:=({\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}})^{\dagger}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}},\qquad\quad{\mathsf{P}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}}={\mathbf{I}}-{\mathsf{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{F}}}.

We can decompose the feature
{\boldsymbol{g}}
with respect to the orthogonal sum of these two subspaces

{\boldsymbol{g}}={\mathsf{P}}_{{\boldsymbol{F}}}{\boldsymbol{g}}+{\mathsf{P}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}}{\boldsymbol{g}}=\sqrt{p}({\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}})^{\dagger}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{z}}+{\mathsf{P}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}}{\boldsymbol{g}}.

We define
{\boldsymbol{r}}:={\mathsf{P}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}}{\boldsymbol{g}}
and
{\boldsymbol{R}}=[{\boldsymbol{r}}_{1},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{r}}_{n}]^{\mathsf{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\infty}
. Similarly, we can decompose the target function

h_{*}({\boldsymbol{g}})=\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},{\boldsymbol{g}}\rangle=\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\boldsymbol{F}},{\boldsymbol{z}}\rangle+\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}},{\boldsymbol{r}}\rangle,

where we introduced

{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}:=\sqrt{p}{\boldsymbol{F}}({\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}})^{\dagger}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},\qquad\qquad{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}}={\mathsf{P}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}.

Note that in particular
\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{z}}\langle{\boldsymbol{r}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}}\rangle]={\boldsymbol{0}}
by orthogonality.

Proof of Theorem
B.12
.

Step 0: Decomposing the bias term.

Note that using the notations introduced above, we can decompose the bias term into

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*};{\boldsymbol{G}},{\boldsymbol{F}},\lambda)=

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}-p^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}+\lambda)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{G}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\left\|{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}\left({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{{\boldsymbol{F}}}-({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}+\lambda)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}({\boldsymbol{Z}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{{\boldsymbol{F}}}+{\boldsymbol{R}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}})\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}+\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T_{1}-2T_{2}+T_{3}+\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}}\|_{2}^{2}.

where we denoted

\displaystyle T_{1}:=

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{{\boldsymbol{F}}},({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}+\lambda)^{-1}\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}+\lambda)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{{\boldsymbol{F}}}\rangle,

\displaystyle T_{2}:=

\displaystyle\lambda\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{{\boldsymbol{F}}},({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}+\lambda)^{-1}\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}+\lambda)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{R}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}}\rangle,

\displaystyle T_{3}:=

\displaystyle\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}},{\boldsymbol{R}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}+\lambda)^{-1}\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}+\lambda)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{R}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}}\rangle.

We proceed similarly to the proof for the variance term, by first considering the deterministic equivalent over
{\boldsymbol{Z}}
conditional on
{\boldsymbol{F}}
, and then over
{\boldsymbol{F}}
. We omit some repetitive details for the sake of brevity.

Step 1: Deterministic equivalent over
{\boldsymbol{Z}}
conditional on
{\boldsymbol{F}}
.

First note that, denoting
\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*}=\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}^{-1/2}
, we have

T_{1}=\lambda^{2}\Phi_{3}({\boldsymbol{Z}};\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*},\lambda).

Furthermore,
T_{3}
and
T_{2}
correspond respectively to the terms (
90
) and (
91
) in Proposition
B.7
with
{\boldsymbol{v}}={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}
and
{\boldsymbol{u}}={\boldsymbol{R}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}}
where

\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}u_{i}]=\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}\langle{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}}\rangle]=0,\qquad\quad\mathbb{E}[u_{i}^{2}]=\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}}\|_{2}^{2}.

Thus, under the assumptions of Theorem
B.12
, we can apply Propositions
B.8
and
B.7
to obtain (via union bound) that with probability at least
1-n^{-D}-p^{-D}
,

\displaystyle\left|T_{1}-(n\nu_{1})^{2}\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*},p\nu_{1})\right|\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(p,n)\cdot(n\nu_{1})^{2}\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*},p\nu_{1}),

\displaystyle\left|T_{2}\right|\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(p,n)\cdot\sqrt{\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot(n\nu_{1})^{2}\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*},p\nu_{1})},

\displaystyle\left|T_{3}-\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot n\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})\right|\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(p,n)\cdot\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}}\|_{2}^{2}.

Hence we deduce that

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{B}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*};{\boldsymbol{G}},{\boldsymbol{F}},\lambda)-(n\nu_{1})^{2}\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*},p\nu_{1})-\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot n\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})-\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}}\|_{2}^{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\cdot\left\{{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(n,p)+{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(n,p)\right\}\cdot\left[(n\nu_{1})^{2}\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*},p\nu_{1})+\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}}\|_{2}^{2}\right].

Let us simplify these terms. Recall that

n\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*},p\nu_{1})=\frac{\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*},p\nu_{1})}{n-\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})},\qquad\quad n\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})\frac{\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})}{n-\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})}.

For the term involving
\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*}
, we can rewrite it as

\displaystyle\nu_{1}^{2}\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*},p\nu_{1})=

\displaystyle\nu_{1}^{2}\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\boldsymbol{F}},\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}^{-1}({\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}})^{2}({\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}+p\nu_{1})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(p\nu_{1})^{2}\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},({\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}})^{\dagger}({\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}})({\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}+p\nu_{1})^{-2}({\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}})({\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}})^{\dagger}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(p\nu_{1})^{2}\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},({\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}+p\nu_{1})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle-\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}}\|_{2}^{2}.

Hence the terms involving
\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}}\|_{2}^{2}
cancel out and we obtain

(n\nu_{1})^{2}\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};\tilde{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*},p\nu_{1})+\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot n\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})+\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\perp,{\boldsymbol{F}}}\|_{2}^{2}=(p\nu_{1})^{2}\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},({\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}+p\nu_{1})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle}{1-\frac{1}{n}\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})}.

Combining the above displays, we deduce that with probability at least
1-n^{-D}-p^{-D}
,

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{B}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*};{\boldsymbol{G}},{\boldsymbol{F}},\lambda)-(p\nu_{1})^{2}\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},({\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}+p\nu_{1})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle}{1-\frac{1}{n}\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})}\right|

(116)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\cdot\left\{{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(n,p)+{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(n,p)\right\}\cdot(p\nu_{1})^{2}\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},({\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}+p\nu_{1})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle}{1-\frac{1}{n}\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})}.

Step 2: Deterministic equivalents over
{\boldsymbol{F}}
.

Following the same steps as Eq. (
110
) in the proof of Theorem
B.10
, we have with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
,

\left|(1-n^{-1}\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1}))^{-1}-(1-\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2}))^{-1}\right|\leq C_{*,D,K}\cdot\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p){\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\cdot(1-\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2}))^{-1}.

Furthermore, from Lemma
B.13
stated below, with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
,

\left|(p\nu_{1})^{2}\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},({\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}+p\nu_{1})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle-\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{n,p}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},\lambda)\right|\leq C_{*,D,K}\cdot\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)^{2}{\mathcal{E}}_{4}(p)\cdot\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{n,p}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},\lambda),

where
\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{n,p}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},\lambda)
is defined in Eq. (
118
). Combining these two bounds and recalling conditions (
114
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left|(p\nu_{1})^{2}\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},({\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}+p\nu_{1})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle}{1-\frac{1}{n}\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\nu_{1})}-\frac{\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{n,p}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},\lambda)}{1-\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\left\{\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p){\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)+\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)^{2}{\mathcal{E}}_{4}(p)\right\}\cdot\frac{\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{n,p}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},\lambda)}{1-\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}.

Noting that
\mathsf{B}_{n,p}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},\lambda)=\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{n,p}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},\lambda)/(1-\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2}))
, we can combine this bound with Eq. (
116
) to obtain via union bound that with probability at least
1-n^{-D}-p^{-D}
,

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{B}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*};{\boldsymbol{G}},{\boldsymbol{F}},\lambda)-\mathsf{B}_{n,p}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},\lambda)\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\left\{{\mathcal{E}}_{1}(n,p)+{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(n,p)+\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p){\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)+\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)^{2}{\mathcal{E}}_{4}(p)\right\}\cdot\mathsf{B}_{n,p}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},\lambda).

Replacing the rates
{\mathcal{E}}_{j}
by their expressions conclude the proof of this theorem.
∎

Lemma B.13
.

Under the setting of Theorem
B.12
and assuming the same conditions (
114
), we have with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
,

\displaystyle\left|(p\nu_{1})^{2}\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},({\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}+p\nu_{1})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle-\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{n,p}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},\lambda)\right|\leq

\displaystyle~C_{*,D,K}\cdot\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)^{2}{\mathcal{E}}_{4}(p)\cdot\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{n,p}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},\lambda),

(117)

where
{\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{4}(p)
are the approximation rates defined in Eqs. (
100
) and (
103
), and we denoted

\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{n,p}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},\lambda):=\nu_{2}^{2}\Big[\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle+\chi(\nu_{2})\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle\Big].

(118)

The proof of Lemma
B.13
can be found in Section
B.7.5
.

B.7
Technical results

In this section, we prove the technical results that were deferred from the previous sections. We start with a lemma that gathers useful bounds on the deterministic functionals.

Lemma B.14
.

There exists a constant
C_{*}>0
such that

\displaystyle(1-\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2}))^{-1}\leq

\displaystyle~C_{*}\cdot\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p),

(119)

where we recall that
\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})
is defined as per Eq. (
105
).
Furthermore, under Assumption
3.2
, we have

\displaystyle p\nu_{1}\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})}{p-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})}\leq

\displaystyle~C_{*}\cdot n\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2}),

(120)

\displaystyle p\nu_{1}\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle}{p-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})}\leq

\displaystyle~C_{*}\cdot\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{n,p}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},\lambda),

(121)

where
\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{n,p}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},\lambda)
is defined as per Eq. (
118
) in Lemma
B.13
.

Proof of Lemma
B.14
.

Step 1: Equation (
119
).

Note that we have

\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})\leq\frac{1}{n}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-1})\leq\frac{p}{n}.

In particular, if
n\geq p/\eta_{*}
, then we can simply write

(1-\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2}))^{-1}\leq\frac{1}{1-\eta_{*}}=C_{*}.

For
n\leq p/\eta_{*}
, note that using the first identity in Eq. (
76
), we have

(1-\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2}))^{-1}\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{n}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-1})\right)^{-1}\leq\frac{n\nu_{1}}{\lambda}\leq C_{*}\rho_{\lambda}(n).

Combining the previous two displays, we obtain Eq. (
119
).

Step 2: Equation (
120
).

We rewrite the left-hand side as

\displaystyle p\nu_{1}\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})}{p-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})}\leq

\displaystyle\frac{p\nu_{1}}{p-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-1})}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-1})-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{1}{{\sf C}_{*}}\right){\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-1}),

where we uses the second of the identities (
76
) in the second line, and Assumption
3.2
in the third line. Hence,

\displaystyle p\nu_{1}\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})}{p-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})}\leq

\displaystyle({\sf C}_{*}-1)\cdot\left\{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-1})-p\nu_{1}\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})}{p-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\sf C}_{*}-1)\cdot n\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2}).

Step 3: Equation (
121
).

Similarly, we get again using Eq. (
76
) and Assumption
3.2
that

\displaystyle p\nu_{1}\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle}{p-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})}\leq

\displaystyle\nu_{2}\left\{\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle-\nu_{2}\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle\right\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{1}{{\sf C}_{*}}\right)\cdot\nu_{2}\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle,

and therefore

\displaystyle p\nu_{1}\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle}{p-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})}\leq

\displaystyle({\sf C}_{*}-1)\left\{\nu_{2}\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle-p\nu_{1}\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle}{p-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{n,p}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},\lambda),

which concludes the proof of this lemma.
∎

B.7.1
Feature covariance matrix

Proof of Lemma
B.2
.

Recall that we consider
\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}=p^{-1}{\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}
, with
{\boldsymbol{F}}=[\boldsymbol{f}_{1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{f}_{p}]^{\mathsf{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times\infty}
and the
\boldsymbol{f}_{i}
are i.i.d. random vectors satisfying Assumption
B.1
. For any integers
k_{2}\geq k_{1}\geq 1
, we split the weight feature matrix into
{\boldsymbol{F}}=[{\boldsymbol{F}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{F}}_{2},{\boldsymbol{F}}_{3}]
, where
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{1}=[\boldsymbol{f}_{1,1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{f}_{1,p}]^{\mathsf{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times k_{1}}
with
\boldsymbol{f}_{1,j}
the first
k_{1}
coordinates of the feature vector
\boldsymbol{f}_{j}
,
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{2}=[\boldsymbol{f}_{2,1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{f}_{2,p}]^{\mathsf{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times(k_{2}-k_{1})}
with
\boldsymbol{f}_{2,j}
the next
k_{2}-k_{1}
coordinates of
\boldsymbol{f}_{j}
, and
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{3}=[\boldsymbol{f}_{3,1},\ldots,\boldsymbol{f}_{3,p}]^{\mathsf{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times\infty}
contains the rest of the coordinates. In other words, we split the feature vector into
\boldsymbol{f}_{j}=[\boldsymbol{f}_{1,j},\boldsymbol{f}_{2,j},\boldsymbol{f}_{3,j}]
. Denote

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{1}=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{f}_{1,j}\boldsymbol{f}_{1,j}^{\mathsf{T}}]={\rm diag}(\xi_{1}^{2},\ldots,\xi_{k_{1}}^{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}\times k_{1}},

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{2}=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{f}_{2,j}\boldsymbol{f}_{2,j}^{\mathsf{T}}]={\rm diag}(\xi_{k_{1}+1}^{2},\ldots,\xi_{k_{2}}^{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{(k_{2}-k_{1})\times(k_{2}-k_{1})},

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{3}=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{f}_{3,j}\boldsymbol{f}_{3,j}^{\mathsf{T}}]={\rm diag}(\xi_{k_{2}+1}^{2},\xi_{k_{2}+2}^{2},\ldots)\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty\times\infty}.

We decompose the feature covariance matrix into

\frac{{\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}}{p}=\frac{{\boldsymbol{F}}_{1}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}}{p}+\frac{{\boldsymbol{F}}_{2}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{2}^{\mathsf{T}}}{p}+\frac{{\boldsymbol{F}}_{3}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{3}^{\mathsf{T}}}{p}.

(122)

Step 1: Bounding the eigenvalues of
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{1}
and
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{2}
.

Introduce the whitened matrices

\overline{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{1}={\boldsymbol{F}}_{1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{1}^{-1/2}=[\overline{\boldsymbol{f}}_{1,1},\ldots,\overline{\boldsymbol{f}}_{1,p}]^{\mathsf{T}},\qquad\quad\overline{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{2}={\boldsymbol{F}}_{2}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{2}^{-1/2}=[\overline{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2,1},\ldots,\overline{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2,p}]^{\mathsf{T}},

so that the feature vectors
\overline{\boldsymbol{f}}_{1,j}
and
\overline{\boldsymbol{f}}_{2,j}
have covariance
{\mathbf{I}}_{k_{1}}
and
{\mathbf{I}}_{k_{2}-k_{1}}
respectively. We have

\|\overline{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{1}/p-{\mathbf{I}}_{k_{1}}\|_{\rm op}=\sup_{{\boldsymbol{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}},\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\|_{2}=1}\left|\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j\in[p]}\langle{\boldsymbol{v}},\overline{\boldsymbol{f}}_{1,j}\rangle^{2}-1\right|

Denote
Z_{j}:=\langle{\boldsymbol{v}},\overline{\boldsymbol{f}}_{1,j}\rangle^{2}-1
. Then we have from Equation (
60
) applied to
{\boldsymbol{B}}={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{1}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{v}}{\boldsymbol{v}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{1}^{-1/2}
, for any integer
q\geq 1
,

\mathbb{E}[|Z_{j}|^{q}]\leq q{\sf C}_{*}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{q-1}e^{-{\sf c}_{x}t}{\rm d}t=\frac{{\sf C}_{*}q!}{{\sf c}_{*}^{q}}.

Hence we can apply Bernstein’s inequality for centered sub-exponential random variable and obtain

\mathbb{P}\left(\Big|\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j\in[p]}Z_{j}\Big|\geq\varepsilon/2\right)\leq 2\exp\left\{-c_{*}p\cdot\min(\varepsilon^{2},\varepsilon)\right\}.

(123)

Following the proof of
[
Vershynin, 2010
, Theorem 5.39]
, we deduce that there exist constants
C_{*},C_{*,D}>0
such that with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
,

\|\overline{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{1}/p-{\mathbf{I}}_{k_{1}}\|_{\rm op}\leq C_{*}\sqrt{\frac{k_{1}}{p}}+C_{*,D}\sqrt{\frac{\log(p)}{p}}.

In particular, there exists
\eta_{*}\in(0,1/4)
and
C_{*,D}>0
such that for
p\geq C_{*,D}
and via union bound, we have with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
(reparametrizing
D
), that for any
k_{1}\leq\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot p\rfloor
,

\|\overline{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{1}/p-{\mathbf{I}}_{k_{1}}\|_{\rm op}\leq 1/2.

From Eq. (
122
), we deduce that the
k_{1}
-th eigenvalue of
\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}
for
k_{1}<\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot p\rfloor
is lower bounded by

\hat{\xi}_{k_{1}}^{2}\geq\lambda_{\min}\left(\frac{{\boldsymbol{F}}_{1}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}}{p}\right)\geq\xi_{k_{1}}^{2}\cdot\lambda_{\min}\left(\frac{\overline{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{1}\overline{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}}{p}\right)\geq\frac{\xi_{k_{1}}^{2}}{2},

(124)

and

\lambda_{\max}\left(\frac{{\boldsymbol{F}}_{1}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}}{p}\right)\leq\frac{3}{2}\xi_{1}^{2}=\frac{3}{2}.

Similarly for
\overline{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{2}
, we have with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
that for any
k_{1}<k_{2}\leq\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot p\rfloor
,

\|\overline{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{2}^{\mathsf{T}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{F}}}_{2}/p-{\mathbf{I}}_{k_{2}-k_{1}}\|_{\rm op}\leq 1/2,

and therefore

\lambda_{\max}\left(\frac{{\boldsymbol{F}}_{2}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{2}^{\mathsf{T}}}{p}\right)\leq\frac{3}{2}\xi_{k_{1}+1}^{2}.

(125)

Step 2: Bounding the eigenvalues of
{\boldsymbol{F}}
.

Equation (
124
) provides a lower bound on the eigenvalues
\hat{\xi}_{k}^{2}
of
\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}
up to
k<\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot p\rfloor
. Let’s upper bound the
p
eigenvalues of
\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}
. From now on, set
k_{2}=p_{*}-1
where
p_{*}:=\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot p\rfloor
.

For the contribution of
\|{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}\|
, we use the matrix Bernstein’s inequality as in
[
Misiakiewicz and Saeed, 2024
, Lemma 1]
(see proof of Theorem
A.2
in Appendix
A
) and obtain that for
p\geq C_{K}
, with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
,

\frac{1}{p}\|{\boldsymbol{F}}_{3}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{3}^{\mathsf{T}}\|_{\rm op}\leq C_{*,D,K}\cdot\xi_{p_{*}}^{2}\left\{1+\frac{r_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}(p_{*})\vee p}{p}\log\left(r_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}(p_{*})\vee p\right)\right\}.

By the min-max theorem, we have with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
for all
k_{1}<p_{*}-1
,

\hat{\xi}_{k_{1}+1}^{2}\leq\lambda_{\max}\left(\frac{{\boldsymbol{F}}_{2}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{2}^{\mathsf{T}}}{p}+\frac{{\boldsymbol{F}}_{3}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{3}^{\mathsf{T}}}{p}\right)\leq\frac{3}{2}\xi_{k_{1}+1}^{2}+C_{*,D,K}\cdot\xi_{p_{*}}^{2}\left\{1+\frac{r_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}(p_{*})\vee p}{p}\log\left(r_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}(p_{*})\vee p\right)\right\},

where we used Eq. (
125
). For
k\geq p_{*}
, we simply use that

\hat{\xi}_{k}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{p}\|{\boldsymbol{F}}_{3}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{3}^{\mathsf{T}}\|_{\rm op}\leq\frac{3}{2}\xi_{k}^{2}+C_{*,D,K}\cdot\xi_{p_{*}}^{2}\left\{1+\frac{r_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}(p_{*})\vee p}{p}\log\left(r_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}(p_{*})\vee p\right)\right\}.

We deduce from the above two displays that with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
, we have for any
k\leq p

\hat{\xi}_{k}^{2}\leq C_{*,K,D}\cdot\left\{\xi_{k}^{2}+\xi_{p_{*}}^{2}\cdot M_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}(p)\right\},

(126)

where we recall that we defined

M_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}(k):=1+\frac{r_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}(\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot k\rfloor)\vee k}{k}\log\left(r_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}(\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot k\rfloor)\vee k\right).

Step 3: Bounding
r_{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}}(k)
for
k\leq p
.

Recall that the intrinsic dimension
r_{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}}(k)
at level
k\leq p
is given by

r_{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}}(k)=\frac{\sum_{j=k}^{p}\hat{\xi}_{j}^{2}}{\hat{\xi}_{k}^{2}}.

First note that for
p\geq k\geq\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot p\rfloor
, we simply use that and the eigenvalues are nonincreasing to get

\frac{\sum_{j=k}^{p}\hat{\xi}_{j}^{2}}{\hat{\xi}_{k}^{2}}\leq(p+1-k)\leq C(1-\eta_{*})p\leq C\frac{1-\eta_{*}}{\eta_{*}}k.

For
k\leq p_{*}-1
, we use that from Eq. (
124
) with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
,

\frac{\sum_{j=k}^{p}\hat{\xi}_{j}^{2}}{\hat{\xi}_{k}^{2}}\leq\frac{2}{\xi_{k}^{2}}\left(\frac{3}{2}\sum_{j=k}^{\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot p\rfloor-1}\xi_{k}^{2}+\sum_{j=\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot p\rfloor}^{p}\hat{\xi}_{j}^{2}\right).

Let’s bound the second term on the right-hand side. Notice that

\sum_{j=\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot p\rfloor+1}^{p}\hat{\xi}_{j}^{2}=\min_{{\boldsymbol{V}}}{\rm Tr}(\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}({\mathbf{I}}-{\boldsymbol{V}}{\boldsymbol{V}}^{\mathsf{T}}))\leq\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{3}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{3}^{\mathsf{T}}),

where the minimization is over
{\boldsymbol{V}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times(\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot p\rfloor-1)}
with
{\boldsymbol{V}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{V}}={\mathbf{I}}_{\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot p\rfloor-1}
and the second inequality is obtained by taking
{\boldsymbol{V}}
orthogonal to the matrix
[{\boldsymbol{F}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{F}}_{2}]
. We can rewrite

\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{3}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{3}^{\mathsf{T}})-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{3})=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j\in[p]}\|\boldsymbol{f}_{3,j}\|_{2}^{2}-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{3}).

Introduce
Z_{j}:=\|\boldsymbol{f}_{3,j}\|_{2}^{2}-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{3})
. By Assumption
B.1
with
{\boldsymbol{B}}={\rm diag}({\boldsymbol{0}},{\mathbf{I}})
(identity on the subspace
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{3}
and
0
otherwise), we have

\mathbb{E}[|Z_{j}|^{q}]\leq q{\sf C}_{x}\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{3}\|_{F}^{q}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{q-1}e^{-{\sf c}_{x}t}{\rm d}t\leq{\sf C}_{x}\cdot{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{3})^{q}\frac{q!}{{\sf c}_{x}^{q}}.

We therefore we can apply Bernstein’s inequality (
123
) again and we get

\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j\in[p]}Z_{j}\right|\geq t\cdot{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{3})\right)\leq 2\exp( - c_* \min(pt^2,pt) ).

Hence, for any
p\geq C_{*,D}
with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
,

\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{3}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{3}^{\mathsf{T}})\leq 2{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{3}).

Combining the above display with the previous inequalities yields with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
that for any
k\leq\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot p\rfloor
,

r_{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}}(k)=\frac{\sum_{j=k}^{p}\hat{\xi}_{j}^{2}}{\hat{\xi}_{k}^{2}}\leq C\frac{\sum_{j=k}^{\infty}\xi_{j}^{2}}{\xi_{k}^{2}}.

We deduce that there exist a constant
C_{*}>0
such that with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
, we have for any
n_{*}:=\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot n\rfloor\leq p

r_{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}}(n_{*})\vee n\leq C_{*}\cdot r_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}(n_{*})\vee n,

(127)

where
r_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}(n)
is the effective rank associated to
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
.

Step 4: Concluding the proof.

For any
n_{*}=\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot n\rfloor>p
, we have
\widehat{\rho}_{\lambda}(n)=\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)=1
. Hence, we only need to consider the case
n_{*}\leq p
. Using Eqs. (
126
) and (
127
), we get

\displaystyle\widehat{\rho}_{\lambda}(n)=

\displaystyle 1+\frac{n\hat{\xi}_{n_{*}}^{2}}{\lambda}\left\{1+\frac{r_{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}}(n_{*})\vee n}{n}\log\left(r_{\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}}(n_{*})\vee n\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 1+C_{*,D,K}\cdot\left\{\frac{n\xi_{n_{*}}^{2}}{\lambda}+\frac{n}{p}\cdot\frac{p\cdot\xi_{p_{*}}}{\lambda}M_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}(p)\right\}M_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}(n)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 1+C_{*,D,K}\cdot\left\{\frac{n\xi_{n_{*}}^{2}}{\lambda}+\frac{n}{p}\cdot\rho_{\lambda}(p)\right\}M_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}(n),

which concludes the proof.
∎

B.7.2
Concentration of the fixed points

Proof of Proposition
B.4
.

Recall that
(\tilde{\nu}_{1},\tilde{\nu}_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2}
are the unique solution to the random fixed point equations (
77
). From the first equation, we have the following bounds on
\tilde{\nu}_{1}
:

\frac{p\lambda}{n}\leq p\tilde{\nu}_{1}\leq\frac{p\|\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}\|_{\rm op}+p\lambda}{n}.

From Lemma
B.2
, we have with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
that
\|\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}\|_{\rm op}\leq C_{*,D,K}\leq p
. Hence, by the uniform concentration over
\kappa\in[p\lambda/n,(p^{3}+p\lambda)/n]
in Lemma
B.15
stated below and an union bound, we deduce that with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
,

\displaystyle\left|{\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}+p\tilde{\nu}_{1})^{-1}\big)-{\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\tilde{\nu}_{2})^{-1}\big)\right|\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,K,D}\frac{\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)^{5/2}\log^{3}(p)}{\sqrt{p}}{\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\tilde{\nu}_{2})^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathcal{E}}}_{2}\cdot{\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\tilde{\nu}_{2})^{-1}\big).

Therefore, we can rewrite the fixed equations (
77
) as

\displaystyle n-\frac{\lambda}{\tilde{\nu}_{1}}=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\tilde{\nu}_{2}\big)^{-1}\big)\cdot(1+\delta({\boldsymbol{F}})),

\displaystyle p-\frac{p\tilde{\nu}_{1}}{\tilde{\nu}_{2}}=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle Tr}\left({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\tilde{\nu}_{2})^{-1}\right).

where with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
, we have
|\delta({\boldsymbol{F}})|\leq\widetilde{{\mathcal{E}}}(p)
. Therefore, by condition (
78
) and
p\geq C_{*,D,K}

C_{*}\widetilde{{\mathcal{E}}}(p)\cdot\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)\leq\frac{C_{*,D,K}}{\log(p)}\leq\frac{1}{2},

and we can directly use Lemma
B.17
stated below to obtain with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
,

\frac{|\tilde{\nu}_{1}-\nu_{1}|}{\nu_{1}}\leq C_{*}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(p)\cdot\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p),\qquad\qquad\frac{|\tilde{\nu}_{2}-\nu_{2}|}{\nu_{2}}\leq C_{*}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(p)\cdot\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p).

This concludes the proof of this proposition.
∎

For any
\kappa\geq 0
, denote
\nu_{2}(\kappa)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
the unique positive solution to

p-\frac{\kappa}{\nu_{2}(\kappa)}={\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}(\kappa))^{-1}\big).

(128)

We will further define analogously to Eq. (
80
) the truncated fixed point

p-\frac{\gamma(\kappa)}{\nu_{2,0}(\kappa)}={\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2,0}(\kappa))^{-1}\big),

(129)

where we recall that we denoted
\gamma(\kappa)=\kappa+{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})
. It will be convenient to recall the notations

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};\kappa)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}+\kappa)^{-1}\big),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};\gamma(\kappa))=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}+\gamma(\kappa))^{-1}\big),

and the deterministic equivalents

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}(\kappa))=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}(\kappa))^{-1}\big),

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0}(\kappa))=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2,0}(\kappa))^{-1}\big).

The next lemma show that
\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}},\kappa)
concentrates on
\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}(\kappa))
uniformly on an interval of
\kappa
.

Lemma B.15
.

Under the setting of Proposition
B.4
and for any
D,K\geq 0
, there exist constants
\eta_{*}\in(0,1/4)
and
C_{*,D,K}>0
such that the following holds. For any
p\geq C_{*,D,K}
and
\lambda>0
, if it holds that

\gamma_{\lambda}=\gamma(p\lambda/n)\geq p^{-K},\qquad\qquad\rho_{\gamma_{\lambda}}(p)^{5/2}\log^{3/2}(p)\leq K\sqrt{p},

(130)

then with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
, we have for any
\kappa\in[p\lambda/n,p(p^{2}+\lambda)/n]
,

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};\kappa)-\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}(\kappa))\right|\leq C_{*,D,K}\frac{\rho_{\gamma(\kappa)}(p)^{5/2}\log^{3}(p)}{\sqrt{p}}\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}(\kappa)).

(131)

Proof of Lemma
B.15
.

Throughout the proof we assume that we are working on the event

\|{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}^{\mathsf{T}}-\gamma(0){\mathbf{I}}_{p}\|_{\rm op}\leq C_{*,D}\frac{\log^{3}(p)}{\sqrt{p}}\gamma(p\lambda/n),

which happens with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
by Lemma
B.3
via union bound. Note that
\gamma(\kappa)\geq\gamma(p\lambda/n)
for all the
\kappa\in[p\lambda/n,p(p^{2}+\lambda)/n]
. Furthermore, we assume that
p\geq C_{*,D}
chosen large enough so that
\|{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}^{\mathsf{T}}-\gamma(0){\mathbf{I}}_{p}\|_{\rm op}\leq 1/2\cdot\gamma(p\lambda/n)
so that

{\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}+\kappa\succeq\frac{1}{2}\gamma(\kappa).

The proof will proceed via a standard union bound argument over
\kappa
in the interval
[p\lambda/n,p(p^{2}+\lambda)/n]
. Let us first prove Eq. (
131
) for a fixed
\kappa
. We first simplify the functional by rewriting it as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};\kappa)=

\displaystyle 1-\frac{\kappa}{p}{\rm Tr}\big(({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}+\kappa)^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\frac{\kappa}{p}{\rm Tr}\big(({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}+\gamma(\kappa))^{-1}\big)+\frac{\kappa}{p}\Delta,

where we denoted

\displaystyle|\Delta|=

\displaystyle\left|{\rm Tr}\big(({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}+\kappa)^{-1}\big)-{\rm Tr}\big(({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}+\gamma(\kappa))^{-1}\big)\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|{\rm Tr}\big(({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}+\kappa)^{-1}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{1}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}-\gamma(0){\mathbf{I}}_{p})({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}+\gamma(\kappa))^{-1}\big)\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D}\frac{\log^{3}(p)}{\sqrt{p}}\left|{\rm Tr}\big(({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}+\gamma(\kappa))^{-1}\big)\right|.

Using again the above identity, we rewrite

\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}\big(({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}+\gamma(\kappa))^{-1}\big)=\frac{1}{\gamma(\kappa)}-\frac{1}{\gamma(\kappa)}\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};\gamma(\kappa)).

We can now apply Eq. (
55
) in Theorem
A.2
: under the assumption of Proposition
B.4
and recalling the conditions (
130
), we have with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
,

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};\gamma(\kappa))-\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0}(\kappa))\right|\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\frac{\rho_{\gamma(\kappa)}(p)^{5/2}\log^{3/2}(p)}{\sqrt{p}}\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0}(\kappa)).

Combining the above displays, we obtain that with probability at least
1-p^{-D}

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};\kappa)-\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0}(\kappa))-\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})}{p\nu_{2,0}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{\gamma(\kappa)}\left|\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};\gamma(\kappa))-\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0}(\kappa))\right|+\kappa|\Delta|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\frac{\rho_{\gamma(\kappa)}(p)^{5/2}\log^{3/2}(p)}{\sqrt{p}}\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0}(\kappa))+C_{*,D}\frac{\log^{3}(p)}{\sqrt{p}}\left|\frac{\kappa}{p}{\rm Tr}\big(({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}+\gamma(\kappa))^{-1}\big)\right|,

where we used in the first inequality identity (
129
) to get

\left(1-\frac{\kappa}{\gamma(\kappa)}\right)\left(1-\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2,0})^{-1})\right)=\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})}{p\nu_{2,0}}.

Further note that using condition (
130
), we can simplify the right-hand side

\displaystyle\left|\kappa{\rm Tr}\big(({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}+\gamma(\kappa))^{-1}\big)\right|\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{\gamma(\kappa)}\left|1-\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0}(\kappa))\right|+C_{*,D,K}\cdot K\cdot\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0}(\kappa))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\left\{\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0}(\kappa))+\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})}{p\nu_{2,0}}\right\}.

We can now use Eq. (
82
) in Lemma
B.5
applied to
\nu_{2}(\kappa)
and
\nu_{2,0}(\kappa)
to concluded that with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
,

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};\kappa)-\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}(\kappa))\right|\leq

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathcal{E}}}(p)\cdot\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}(\kappa)),\qquad\widetilde{{\mathcal{E}}}(p):=C_{*,D,K}\frac{\rho_{\gamma(\kappa)}(p)^{5/2}\log^{3}(p)}{\sqrt{p}}.

(132)

We now consider a
p^{-P}
-grid
{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
of the interval
\kappa\in[p\lambda/n,p(p^{2}+\lambda)/n]
, which contains at most
p^{P+3}
points. We can use a union bound over
\kappa\in{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
and reparametrizing
D^{\prime}=D+P+3
, so that with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
, Equation (
132
) holds for any
\kappa\in{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
. Then for any point
\kappa_{1}\in[p\lambda/n,p(Kp^{2}+\lambda)/n]
, denote
\kappa_{0}\in{\mathcal{P}}_{n}
its closest point. Then by Lemma
B.16
stated below, we have

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};\kappa_{1})-\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}(\kappa_{1}))\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};\kappa_{1})-\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};\kappa_{0})\right|+\left|\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};\kappa_{0})-\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}(\kappa_{0}))\right|+\left|\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}(\kappa_{0}))-\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}(\kappa_{1}))\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle p^{CK}|\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{0}|\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};\kappa_{0})+\widetilde{{\mathcal{E}}}(p)\cdot\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}(\kappa_{0}))+p^{CK}|\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{0}|\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}(\kappa_{1}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(p^{CK-P}+\widetilde{{\mathcal{E}}}(p)\right)\left(1+p^{CK-P}+\widetilde{{\mathcal{E}}}(p)\right)\cdot\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}(\kappa_{1})).

where we used that
|\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{0}|\leq p^{-P}
. Taking
P=CK+1
concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma B.16
.

Under the setting of Proposition
B.4
and for any
D,K\geq 0
, there exist constants
\eta_{*}\in(0,1/4)
,
C>0
and
C_{*,D}>0
such that the following holds. For any
p\geq 1
and
\lambda>0
, it holds that

\gamma(p\lambda/n)=\frac{p\lambda}{n}+{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})\geq p^{-K},

(133)

then for any
\kappa_{0},\kappa_{1}\geq p\lambda/n
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}(\kappa_{1}))}{\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}(\kappa_{0}))}-1\right|\leq p^{CK}|\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{0}|.

(134)

Furthermore, if
p\geq C_{*,D}
, then we have with probability
1-p^{-D}
that for any
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\geq p\lambda/n
,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};\kappa_{1})}{\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};\kappa_{0})}-1\right|\leq p^{CK}|\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{0}|.

(135)

Proof of Lemma
B.16
.

Using the identity (
128
), we can decompose the first difference into

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}(\kappa_{1}))}{\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}(\kappa_{0}))}-1\right|=

\displaystyle\frac{\left|\frac{\kappa_{0}}{\nu_{2}(\kappa_{0})}-\frac{\kappa_{1}}{\nu_{2}(\kappa_{1})}\right|}{p\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}(\kappa_{0}))}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\nu_{2}(\kappa_{0})\cdot p\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}(\kappa_{0}))}\left\{|\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{0}|+\left|\frac{\nu_{2}(\kappa_{0})}{\nu_{2}(\kappa_{1})}-1\right|\right\}.

From condition (
133
) and the proof of
[
Misiakiewicz and Saeed, 2024
, Lemma 11]
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\nu_{2}(\kappa_{0})\cdot p\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}(\kappa_{0}))}\leq

\displaystyle p^{3+2K},

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\nu_{2}(\kappa_{0})}{\nu_{2}(\kappa_{1})}-1\right|\leq

\displaystyle p^{2+2K}|\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{0}|.

Combining the above inequalities, we deduce that there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\left|\frac{\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}(\kappa_{1}))}{\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}(\kappa_{0}))}-1\right|\leq p^{CK}|\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{0}|.

For the second inequality (
135
), we rewrite the difference as

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};\kappa_{1})}{\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};\kappa_{0})}-1\right|=

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}+\kappa_{1})^{-1}({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}+\kappa_{0})^{-1})}{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}+\kappa_{0})^{-1})}|\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{0}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}+\kappa_{1})^{-1}\|_{\rm op}|\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{0}|.

Hence, recalling Lemma
B.3
and condition (
133
), for any
p\geq C_{*,D}
, we get with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
and for all
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\geq p\lambda/n
,

\left|\frac{\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};\kappa_{1})}{\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};\kappa_{0})}-1\right|\leq\frac{2}{\gamma(\kappa_{1})}|\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{0}|\leq n^{K}|\kappa_{1}-\kappa_{0}|,

which concludes the proof of this lemma.
∎

We consider
(\nu_{1}^{\varepsilon},\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon})\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
the unique positive solutions of the perturbed equations:

\displaystyle n-\frac{\lambda}{\nu_{1}^{\varepsilon}}=

\displaystyle~{\rm Tr}\left({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon})^{-1}\right)(1+\varepsilon),

(136)

\displaystyle p-\frac{p\nu_{1}^{\varepsilon}}{\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon}}=

\displaystyle~{\rm Tr}\left({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon})^{-1}\right).

(137)

Lemma B.17
.

Let
\eta_{*}\in(0,1/4)
be chosen as in Proposition
B.4
. Then there exists
C_{*},C_{*}^{\prime}>0
such that for any
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}
with

|\varepsilon|\cdot C_{*}\cdot\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)\leq\frac{1}{2},

then

\left|\frac{\nu_{1}^{\varepsilon}-\nu_{1}^{0}}{\nu_{1}^{0}}\right|\leq C_{*}^{\prime}\cdot\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)\cdot|\varepsilon|,\qquad\qquad\left|\frac{\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon}-\nu_{2}^{0}}{\nu_{2}^{0}}\right|\leq C_{*}^{\prime}\cdot\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)\cdot|\varepsilon|.

Proof of Lemma
B.17
.

For convenience, we introduce the notations

\delta_{1}:=\frac{\nu_{1}^{\varepsilon}-\nu_{1}^{0}}{\nu_{1}^{\varepsilon}},\qquad\qquad\delta_{2}:=\frac{\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon}-\nu_{2}^{0}}{\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon}}.

We first consider the second equation (
137
) and subtract the identities for
(\nu_{1}^{\varepsilon},\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon})
and
(\nu_{1}^{0},\nu_{2}^{0})
to obtain

\displaystyle p\left(\frac{\nu_{1}^{0}}{\nu_{2}^{0}}-\frac{\nu_{1}^{\varepsilon}}{\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon}}\right)+{\rm Tr}\left({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{0})^{-1}\right)-{\rm Tr}\left({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon})^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p\left[\frac{\nu_{1}^{0}}{\nu_{2}^{0}}\delta_{2}-\frac{\nu_{1}^{\varepsilon}}{\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon}}\delta_{1}\right]+\delta_{2}\cdot\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{0})^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon})^{-1})=0.

Hence, we obtain the first identity

\delta_{2}=\delta_{1}\frac{(\nu_{1}^{\varepsilon}/\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon})}{(\nu_{1}^{0}/\nu_{2}^{0})+\frac{\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon}}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{0})^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon})^{-1})}.

(138)

We now turn to the first equation (
136
): we obtain similarly

\displaystyle\lambda\left(\frac{1}{\nu_{1}^{0}}-\frac{1}{\nu_{1}^{\varepsilon}}\right)={\rm Tr}\left({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon})^{-1}\right)(1+\varepsilon)-{\rm Tr}\left({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{0})^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda}{\nu_{1}^{0}}\delta_{1}=\delta_{2}\cdot\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{0})^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon})^{-1})(1+\varepsilon)+\varepsilon{\rm Tr}\left({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{0})^{-1}\right).

Hence, rearranging the term and recalling the first identity (
136
), we get the second identity

\delta_{1}\left[\frac{\lambda}{\nu_{1}^{0}}+(1+\varepsilon)\frac{\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{0})^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon})^{-1})\cdot(\nu_{1}^{\varepsilon}/\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon})}{(\nu_{1}^{0}/\nu_{2}^{0})+\frac{\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon}}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{0})^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon})^{-1})}\right]=\varepsilon{\rm Tr}\left({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{0})^{-1}\right).

(139)

From this identity, we directly have

|\delta_{1}|\leq|\varepsilon|\cdot\frac{\nu_{1}^{0}}{\lambda}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{0})^{-1}).

Note that for
n\geq p/\eta_{*}
, we simply have by Eq. (
136
) that

\frac{\nu_{1}^{0}}{\lambda}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{0})^{-1})=\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{0})^{-1})}{n-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{0})^{-1})}\leq\frac{p}{n-p}\leq\frac{\eta_{*}}{1-\eta_{*}},

where we use that
{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{0})^{-1})\leq p
by Eq. (
137
). For
n\leq p/\eta_{*}
, let’s denote
\mu_{1}^{0}=\lambda/\nu_{1}^{0}
. Rewriting Eq. (
136
), we get that

\mu_{*}=\frac{n}{1+{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}(\mu_{*}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\mu_{*}\nu_{2}^{0})^{-1})}.

Hence

\displaystyle\frac{\nu_{1}^{0}}{\lambda}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{0})^{-1})=
