\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{<\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot n\rfloor}(\mu_{1}^{0}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{<\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot n\rfloor}+\mu_{1}^{0}\nu_{2}^{0})^{-1})+{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\geq\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot n\rfloor}(\mu_{1}^{0}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\geq\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot n\rfloor}+\mu_{1}^{0}\nu_{2}^{0})^{-1})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\eta_{*}\cdot n}{\mu_{1}^{0}}+\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\geq\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot n\rfloor})}{\mu_{1}^{0}\nu_{2}^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\eta_{*}\left\{1+{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}(\mu_{*}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\mu_{*}\nu_{2}^{0})^{-1})\right\}+\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\geq\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot n\rfloor})}{\lambda},

where we use in the last inequality that
\nu_{1}^{0}/\nu_{2}^{0}\leq 1
and the definition of the effective rank. Rearranging the terms we obtain

\frac{\nu_{1}^{0}}{\lambda}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{0})^{-1})\leq\frac{1}{1-\eta_{*}}\left\{1+\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\geq\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot n\rfloor})}{\lambda}\right\}.

Combining the above bounds we deduce that

|\delta_{1}|\leq C_{*}|\varepsilon|\cdot\left\{1+\mathbbm{1}_{n\leq p/\eta_{*}}\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\geq\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot n\rfloor})}{\lambda}\right\}\leq C_{*}\cdot\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)\cdot|\varepsilon|.

By assumption
C_{*}\cdot\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)\cdot|\varepsilon|\leq 1/2
and therefore
\nu_{1}^{\varepsilon}\leq 2\nu_{1}^{0}
. We conclude the first inequality

\left|\frac{\nu_{1}^{\varepsilon}-\nu_{1}^{0}}{\nu_{1}^{0}}\right|\leq\frac{\nu_{1}^{\varepsilon}}{\nu_{1}^{0}}|\delta_{1}|\leq C_{*}\cdot\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)\cdot|\varepsilon|.

Recalling Eq. (
138
), we have directly

|\delta_{2}|\leq|\delta_{1}|\frac{\nu_{1}^{\varepsilon}\nu_{2}^{0}}{\nu_{1}^{0}\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon}}\;\;\implies\;\;\left|\frac{\nu_{2}^{\varepsilon}-\nu_{2}^{0}}{\nu_{2}^{0}}\right|\leq\left|\frac{\nu_{1}^{\varepsilon}-\nu_{1}^{0}}{\nu_{1}^{0}}\right|,

which concludes the proof.
∎

B.7.3
Proof of deterministic equivalents for functionals on
\mathbb{Z}

Proof of Proposition
B.6
.

Recall that
\widehat{\rho}_{\lambda}(n)
is defined in Eq. (
72
) and that for
{\boldsymbol{F}}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{F}}
, we have
\widehat{\rho}_{\lambda}(n)\leq C_{*,D,K}\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Under the assumptions of Proposition
B.6
, we can apply Theorem
A.2
to
{\boldsymbol{Z}}
conditional on
{\boldsymbol{F}}
to obtain that with probability at least
1-n^{-D}
,

\displaystyle\left|\Phi_{2}({\boldsymbol{Z}};\lambda)-\frac{p}{n}\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};p\tilde{\nu}_{1})\right|\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)^{5/2}\log^{3/2}(n)}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\frac{p}{n}\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};p\tilde{\nu}_{1}),

\displaystyle\left|\Phi_{3}({\boldsymbol{Z}};{\boldsymbol{A}},\lambda)-\left(\frac{n\tilde{\nu}_{1}}{\lambda}\right)^{2}\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}},p\tilde{\nu}_{1})\right|\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)^{6}\log^{5/2}(n)}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\left(\frac{n\tilde{\nu}_{1}}{\lambda}\right)^{2}\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}},p\tilde{\nu}_{1}),

\displaystyle\left|\Phi_{4}({\boldsymbol{Z}};{\boldsymbol{A}},\lambda)-\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}},p\tilde{\nu}_{1})\right|\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)^{6}\log^{3/2}(n)}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}},p\tilde{\nu}_{1}),

where
\tilde{\nu}_{1}
is the solution of the fixed point equation (
77
). We conclude the proof using Lemma
B.18
stated below and that by condition (
85
), we have
C_{*,D,K}\cdot\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p){\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(p)\leq C_{*,D,K}K
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
B.7
.

Again, under the assumptions of the proposition and for
{\boldsymbol{F}}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{F}}
, we can apply
[
Misiakiewicz and Saeed, 2024
, Lemma 10]
to get

\displaystyle\left|\langle{\boldsymbol{u}},{\boldsymbol{Z}}({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}+\lambda)^{-1}\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}+\lambda)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{u}}\rangle-n\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\tilde{\nu}_{1})\right|\leq C_{*,D,K}\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)^{6}\log^{7/2}(n)}{\sqrt{n}},

\displaystyle\left|\langle{\boldsymbol{u}},{\boldsymbol{Z}}({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}+\lambda)^{-1}\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{Z}}+\lambda)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{v}}\rangle\right|\leq C_{*,D,K}\frac{\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)^{6}\log^{7/2}(n)}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\frac{n\nu_{1}}{\lambda}\sqrt{\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};\overline{{\boldsymbol{v}}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{v}}}^{\mathsf{T}},p\tilde{\nu}_{1})}.

We conclude by combining these inequalities with Lemma
B.18
.
∎

Lemma B.18
.

For
{\boldsymbol{F}}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathcal{F}}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};p\tilde{\nu}_{1})-\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};p\nu_{1})\right|\leq

\displaystyle~C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(p)\cdot\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};p\nu_{1}),

(140)

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}},p\tilde{\nu}_{1})-\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}},p\nu_{1})\right|\leq

\displaystyle~C_{*,D,K}\cdot\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p){\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(p)\cdot\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}},p\nu_{1}).

(141)

and

\displaystyle\left|\left(\frac{n\tilde{\nu}_{1}}{\lambda}\right)^{2}\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}},p\tilde{\nu}_{1})-\left(\frac{n\nu_{1}}{\lambda}\right)^{2}\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}},p\nu_{1})\right|

(142)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\cdot\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p){\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(p)\cdot\left(\frac{n\nu_{1}}{\lambda}\right)^{2}\widetilde{\Phi}_{5}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}},p\nu_{1}).

Proof of Lemma
B.18
.

For convenience, we denote
\kappa:=\nu_{1}
,
\kappa^{\prime}:=\tilde{\nu}_{1}
, and
{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}:=\widehat{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}_{\boldsymbol{F}}
. For Eq. (
140
), we simply use that

\displaystyle\left|p\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};p\tilde{\nu}_{1})-p\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};p\nu_{1})\right|=

\displaystyle\left|{\rm Tr}\left({\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}({\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}+\kappa^{\prime})^{-1}\right)-{\rm Tr}\left({\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}({\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}+\kappa)^{-1}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\kappa-\kappa^{\prime}|{\rm Tr}\left({\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}({\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}+\kappa^{\prime})^{-1}({\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}+\kappa)^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{|\kappa^{\prime}-\kappa|}{\kappa^{\prime}}{\rm Tr}\left({\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}({\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}+\kappa)^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(p)\cdot p\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};p\nu_{1}).

Similarly, by simple algebra, we have

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}},p\tilde{\nu}_{1})-\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}},p\nu_{1})\right|

(143)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|{\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{A}}{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}+\kappa^{\prime})^{-2}\big)-{\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{A}}{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}+\kappa)^{-2}\big)\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2|\kappa-\kappa^{\prime}|{\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{A}}{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}^{3}({\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}+\kappa^{\prime})^{-2}({\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}+\kappa)^{-2}\big)+|\kappa^{2}-(\kappa^{\prime})^{2}|{\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{A}}{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}^{3}({\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}+\kappa^{\prime})^{-2}({\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}+\kappa)^{-2}\big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{|\kappa-\kappa^{\prime}|}{\kappa^{\prime}}\left\{2+\frac{|\kappa+\kappa^{\prime}|}{\kappa^{\prime}}\right\}{\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{A}}{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Gamma}}+\kappa)^{-2}\big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(p)\cdot\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}},p\nu_{1}).

Furthermore, note that by the identity (
77
),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\tilde{\nu}_{1})\left(n-\widetilde{\Phi}_{6}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\mathbf{I}},p\tilde{\nu}_{1})\right)^{-1}\leq\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};p\tilde{\nu}_{1})\left(n-\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};p\tilde{\nu}_{1})\right)^{-1}=\frac{\tilde{\nu}_{1}}{\lambda}\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};p\tilde{\nu}_{1}).

Furthermore, we have from the previous computation and simple algebra that

\frac{\tilde{\nu}_{1}}{\lambda}\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};p\tilde{\nu}_{1})\leq(1+C_{*,D,K}{\mathcal{E}}_{\nu}(p))\cdot\frac{\nu_{1}}{\lambda}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-1})\leq C_{*,D,K}\cdot\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p),

where we used the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
B.17
in the last inequality as well as condition (
85
).
The proof of Eqs. (
142
) and (
141
) from simple algebra from the above displays and (
143
).
∎

B.7.4
Proof of Lemma
B.11

Proof of Lemma
B.11
.

For convenience, we introduce the notations

{\boldsymbol{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{F}}}:=({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}+p\nu_{1})^{-1},\qquad{\boldsymbol{G}}_{0}:=({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}+\gamma_{+})^{-1},\qquad{\boldsymbol{R}}_{0}:=({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}+\gamma_{+})^{-1},

(144)

where we recall that we denoted
\gamma_{+}=\gamma(p\nu_{1})=p\nu_{1}+{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})
.
Recall that for the first approximation guarantee, we denote

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};p\nu_{1})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{F}}}),\qquad\quad

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};p\nu_{1})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{G}}_{0}),

and the deterministic equivalents

\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2})=\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-1}),\qquad\quad\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0})=\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2,0})^{-1}).

For the second approximation guarantee, recall that we denote

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{4}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1},p\nu_{1})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{F}}}^{2}),\qquad\quad

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{4}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{-1},p\nu_{1})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{G}}_{0}^{2}),

and their associated deterministic equivalents

\displaystyle\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\cdot\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})}{p-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}+\nu_{2})^{-2})},\qquad

\displaystyle\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{-1})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\cdot\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2,0})^{-2})}{p-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{2}+\nu_{2,0})^{-2})}.

We separate the analysis of the low-degree part
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}
from the high-degree part
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}
. To remove the dependency on the high-degree part
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}
, we recall that by Lemma
B.3
, we have with probability at least
1-p^{-D}

\|{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}\|_{\rm op}\leq C_{*,D}\frac{\log^{3}(p)}{\sqrt{p}}\gamma_{+},

(145)

where we denoted
{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}:={\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})\cdot{\mathbf{I}}_{p}
and we recall that
\gamma_{+}=\gamma(p\nu_{1})=p\nu_{1}+{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})
.
In particular, taking
p\geq C_{*,D}
, we have
\|{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}\|_{\rm op}\leq\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{+}
and therefore

\|{\boldsymbol{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{F}}}\|_{\rm op}\leq 2\|{\boldsymbol{G}}_{0}\|_{\rm op}\leq\frac{2}{\gamma_{+}}.

(146)

Step 1: Bound on
|\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};p\nu_{1})-\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2})|
.

First note that we have the identity

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};p\nu_{1})=1-\nu_{1}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{F}}})=1-\nu_{1}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{G}}_{0})+\nu_{1}\Theta,

where we denoted
\Theta={\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{G}}_{0})-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{F}}})
. By Eqs. (
145
) and (
146
), we have

\displaystyle\left|\Theta\right|=\left|{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{F}}}{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}{\boldsymbol{G}}_{0})\right|\leq C_{*,D}\frac{\log^{3}(p)}{\sqrt{p}}\cdot{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{G}}_{0}).

(147)

Using again the above identity, we have

\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{G}}_{0})=\frac{1}{\gamma_{+}}-\frac{1}{\gamma_{+}}\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};p\nu_{1}).

(148)

Under the assumption of the lemma, we can apply Proposition
B.8
and obtain with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
that

\left|\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};p\nu_{1})-\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0})\right|\leq C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\cdot\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0}),

(149)

where
{\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)
is defined in Eq. (
100
). Furthermore note that using identity (
80
), we have

\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})}{\gamma_{+}}+\frac{p\nu_{1}}{\gamma_{+}}\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0})=\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0})+\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})}{p\nu_{2,0}},

and that by Eq. (
82
) in Lemma
B.5
,

\left|\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0})+\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})}{p\nu_{2,0}}-\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2})\right|\leq\frac{C}{p}\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}).

(150)

Thus combining Eqs. (
147
), (
149
) and (
150
), we obtain

\displaystyle|\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}};p\nu_{1})-\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2})|\leq

\displaystyle\nu_{1}|\Theta|+\frac{p\nu_{1}}{\gamma_{+}}\left|\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};p\nu_{1})-\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0})\right|+\left|\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0})+\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})}{p\nu_{2,0}}-\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2})\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\left\{\frac{\log^{3}(p)}{\sqrt{p}}+{\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)+\frac{1}{p}\right\}\left[\nu_{1}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{G}}_{0})+\frac{p\nu_{1}}{\gamma_{+}}\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0})+\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2})\right],

with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
via union bound.
Using condition (
106
), we can simplify the right-hand side using identity (
148
) and bounds (
149
) and (
150
) to get

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{G}}_{0})\leq

\displaystyle\frac{p\nu_{1}}{\gamma_{+}}\left|1-\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0})\right|+C_{*,D,K}K\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\left\{\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0})+\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})}{p\nu_{2,0}}\right\}\leq C_{*,D,K}\cdot\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2}).

This concludes the proof of the first part of this lemma.

Step 2: Bound on
|\widetilde{\Phi}_{4}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1},p\nu_{1})-\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1})|
.

We proceed similarly as in the first part and omit some repetitive details. First note that we can rewrite

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{4}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1},p\nu_{1})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{F}}})-\nu_{1}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{F}}}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{G}}_{0})-\nu_{1}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{G}}_{0}^{2})+\Theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})}{\gamma_{+}^{2}}\left(1-\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};p\nu_{1})\right)+\frac{p\nu_{1}}{\gamma_{+}}\widetilde{\Phi}_{4}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{-1},p\nu_{1})+\Theta,

where

\displaystyle|\Theta|=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\left|{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{F}}})-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{G}}_{0})+p\nu_{1}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{G}}_{0}^{2})-p\nu_{1}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{F}}}^{2})\right|

(151)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D}\frac{\log^{3}(p)}{\sqrt{p}}\cdot\left[\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{G}}_{0})+\nu_{1}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{G}}_{0}^{2})\right].

Again, by Proposition
B.8
, we get that with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
that

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\Phi}_{2}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};p\nu_{1})-\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0})\right|\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\cdot\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0}),

(152)

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\Phi}_{4}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{-1},p\nu_{1})-\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{-1})\right|\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\cdot\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{-1}).

Furthermore, note that by Lemma
B.19
stated below, we have

\left|\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})}{\gamma_{+}^{2}}\left(1-\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0})\right)+\frac{p\nu_{1}}{\gamma_{+}}\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{-1})-\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1})\right|\leq C\frac{\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)}{p}\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1}).

(153)

Combining Eqs. (
151
), (
152
) and (
153
), we deduce via union bound that with probability at least
1-p^{-D}

\displaystyle|\widetilde{\Phi}_{4}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1},p\nu_{1})-\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1})|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\left[\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{G}}_{0})+\nu_{1}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{G}}_{0}^{2})+\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})^{2}}{\gamma_{+}^{2}}\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0})+\frac{p\nu_{1}}{\gamma_{+}}\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{-1})+\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1})\right].

Let us simplify the right hand side. First, from the proof of Lemma
B.19
, we have

\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})^{2}}{\gamma_{+}^{2}}\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0})+\frac{p\nu_{1}}{\gamma_{+}}\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{-1})\leq C\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)\cdot\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1}).

Combining the above two displays with
{\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\leq K
from conditions (
106
) yields

\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{G}}_{0})-\nu_{1}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{G}}_{0}^{2})\leq C_{*,D,K}\cdot\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)\cdot\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1}).

Finally, note that using Eq. (
152
) and again
{\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\leq K
that

\nu_{1}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{G}}_{0}^{2})\leq\frac{p\nu_{1}}{\gamma_{+}}\widetilde{\Phi}_{4}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{-1/2},p\nu_{1})\leq C_{*,D,K}\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1}),

which concludes the proof of this lemma.
∎

Lemma B.19
.

Assuming that
p^{2}\xi_{\mathsf{m}}^{2}\leq\gamma_{+}
, we have

\left|\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})}{\gamma_{+}^{2}}\left(1-\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0})\right)+\frac{p\nu_{1}}{\gamma_{+}}\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{-1})-\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1})\right|\leq C\frac{\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)}{p}\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1}).

Proof of Lemma
B.19
.

For convenience, we introduce

\Upsilon(\nu_{2,0}):=\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2,0})^{-2}),\qquad\quad\Upsilon(\nu_{2}):=\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2}).

Using that

\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2,0})^{-2})=\frac{1}{\nu_{2,0}}\left\{\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0})-\Upsilon(\nu_{2,0})\right\},

we obtain by simple algebra and using identity (
80
) that

\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})}{\gamma_{+}^{2}}\left(1-\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0})\right)+\frac{p\nu_{1}}{\gamma_{+}}\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{-1})=\frac{1}{\nu_{2,0}}\frac{\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0})+\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+})}{p\nu_{2,0}}-\Upsilon(\nu_{2,0})}{p(1-\Upsilon(\nu_{2,0}))}.

Following the proof of
[
Misiakiewicz and Saeed, 2024
, Lemma 7]
, we get that

\displaystyle\left|\Upsilon(\nu_{2,0})-\Upsilon(\nu_{2})\right|\leq 10\frac{p\xi_{{\mathsf{m}}}^{2}}{\gamma_{+}}\leq\frac{C}{p},

\displaystyle(1-\Upsilon(\nu_{2,0}))^{-1}\leq(1-\Psi_{2}(\nu_{2,0}))^{-1}=\frac{p\nu_{2,0}}{\gamma_{+}}\leq C\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p).

Recalling Eq. (
82
) in Lemma
B.5
, we can conclude the proof using simple algebraic manipulations similarly to
[
Misiakiewicz and Saeed, 2024
, Lemma 7]
.
∎

B.7.5
Proof of Lemma
B.13

Proof of Lemma
B.13
.

We will follow similar steps as in the proof of Lemma
B.11
. For the sake of brevity, we omit some repetitive details. Recall that we introduced the notations (
144
). We decompose the coefficient vector
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}=[{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}]
with
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{m}}
the first
{\mathsf{m}}
coordinates of
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}
(aligned with the top
{\mathsf{m}}
eigenspaces), while
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
corresponds to the rest of the coordinates. Further introduce the matrices

{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*}:={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{-1/2},\qquad\qquad{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}:={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{-1/2},

and recall the expressions of the functionals

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*},p\nu_{1})=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{A}}_{*}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{R}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\big),\qquad\quad

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0},p\nu_{1})=

\displaystyle\rm{\displaystyle Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{R}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{1/2}\big),

as well as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{4}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*},p\nu_{1})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{A}}_{*}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{R}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{R}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}\big),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{4}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0},p\nu_{1})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}{\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{R}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{R}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{1/2}\big).

We further recall the expressions of the deterministic equivalents

\Psi_{1}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*})={\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{A}}_{*}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-1}\big),\qquad\quad\Psi_{1}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0})={\rm Tr}\big({\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2,0})^{-1}\big),

and

\displaystyle\psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\cdot\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{A}}_{*}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})}{p-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-2})},

\displaystyle\psi_{3}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p}\cdot\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2,0})^{-2})}{p-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2,0})^{-2})}.

We first rewrite our functionals into

\displaystyle(p\nu_{1})^{2}\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},{\boldsymbol{R}}^{2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle=

\displaystyle(p\nu_{1})\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},{\boldsymbol{R}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle-(p\nu_{1})\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},{\boldsymbol{R}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{R}}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(p\nu_{1})\left[\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*},p\nu_{1})-p\widetilde{\Phi}_{4}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*},p\nu_{1})\right],

and study each term separately.

Step 1: Bounding term
\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*},p\nu_{1})
.

Let us start by removing the dependency on the high-degree part
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}
in the denominator. Note that we can rewrite the matrix
{\boldsymbol{R}}
in block matrix form

(p\nu_{1}){\boldsymbol{R}}=(p\nu_{1})\begin{pmatrix}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}+p\nu_{1}&{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}\\
{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}&{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}+p\nu_{1}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}=:\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{R}}}_{00}&\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{R}}}_{0+}\\
\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{R}}}_{0+}^{\mathsf{T}}&\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{R}}}_{++}\end{pmatrix},

so that

(p\nu_{1})\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*},p\nu_{1})={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{R}}}_{00}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}+2{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{R}}}_{0+}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}+{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{R}}}_{++}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}.

Let us study each of these terms separately. Denote
\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{G}}}_{+}:={\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}^{\mathsf{T}}+p\nu_{1}
. By simple algebra, we have

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{R}}}_{00}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}=

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}\left({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{G}}}_{+}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}+1\right)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}=\gamma_{+}\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0},p\nu_{1})+\Theta_{00},

where

\displaystyle|\Theta_{00}|=

\displaystyle\left|\gamma_{+}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{R}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}\left({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{G}}}_{+}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}+1\right)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\left\|{\boldsymbol{R}}_{0}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}({\mathbf{I}}-\gamma_{+}\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{G}}}_{+}){\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{R}}_{0}^{1/2}\right\|_{\rm op}\cdot\gamma_{+}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{R}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D}\frac{\log^{3}(p)}{\sqrt{p}}\gamma_{+}\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0},p\nu_{1}).

Furthermore, from Proposition
B.8
, we have with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
that

\left|\gamma_{+}\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0},p\nu_{1})-\nu_{2,0}\Psi_{1}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0})\right|\leq C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\cdot\nu_{2,0}\Psi_{1}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}).

Hence, combining the previous two displays and using that
{\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\leq K
by conditions (
114
), we have

\left|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{R}}}_{00}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}-\nu_{2,0}\Psi_{1}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0})\right|\leq C_{*,D,K}\cdot\left\{\frac{\log^{3}(p)}{\sqrt{p}}+{\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\right\}\nu_{2,0}\Psi_{1}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}).

(154)

Similarly, denoting
\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{G}}}_{0}=({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}+p\nu_{1})^{-1}
, we can rewrite the third term as

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{R}}}_{++}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}=

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}^{\mathsf{T}}\left({\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{G}}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}+1\right)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}=\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}\|_{2}^{2}-\Theta_{++},

where with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
,

\displaystyle\Theta_{++}=

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{G}}}_{0}^{1/2}\left(\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{G}}}_{0}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{G}}}_{0}^{1/2}+1\right)^{-1}\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{G}}}_{0}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}

(155)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|({\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}^{\mathsf{T}}+\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{G}}}_{0}^{-1})^{-1}\|_{\rm op}\cdot\|{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C}{\gamma_{+}}\cdot p\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{C}{p}\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}\|_{2}^{2},

where we used the same concentration argument as in Step 3 of the proof of Lemma
B.2
.

Finally, we rewrite

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{R}}}_{0+}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}=-{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}\left({\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{G}}}_{+}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}+1\right)^{-1}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}({\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}^{\mathsf{T}}+p\nu_{1})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}.

Hence, using Eqs. (
154
) and (
155
) as well as conditions (
114
), we obtain

\displaystyle|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}\widetilde{{\boldsymbol{R}}}_{0+}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}|\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\left\{\nu_{2,0}\Psi_{1}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0})+\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}\|_{2}^{2}\right\}.

(156)

Finally, note that by Lemma
B.20
stated below, we have

\left|\nu_{2,0}\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0},({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2,0})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}\rangle+\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}\|_{2}^{2}-\nu_{2}\Psi_{1}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*})\right|\leq\frac{C}{p}\nu_{2}\Psi_{1}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*}).

(157)

Combining Eqs. (
154
), (
155
), (
156
), and (
157
), we deduce that
with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
,

\left|(p\nu_{1})\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*},p\nu_{1})-\nu_{2}\Psi_{1}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*})\right|\leq C_{*,D,K}\cdot\left\{\frac{\log^{3}(p)}{\sqrt{p}}+{\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\right\}\cdot\nu_{2}\Psi_{1}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*}).

(158)

Step 2: Bounding term
\widetilde{\Phi}_{4}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*},p\nu_{1})
.

We rewrite this term as

\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},{\boldsymbol{R}}{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{R}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle=\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{G}}_{0}^{2}{\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle+\Theta,

where

\displaystyle|\Theta|=

\displaystyle\left|\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}({\boldsymbol{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{F}}}^{2}-{\boldsymbol{G}}_{0}^{2}){\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle\right|

(159)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{G}}_{0}\left(-2{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}{\boldsymbol{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{F}}}+{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}{\boldsymbol{G}}_{{\boldsymbol{F}}}^{2}{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}\right){\boldsymbol{G}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle\right|\leq C_{*,D,K}\frac{\log^{3}(d)}{p}\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{G}}_{0}^{2}{\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle.

Let us decompose

\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{G}}_{0}^{2}{\boldsymbol{F}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle=\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0},{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{G}}_{0}^{2}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}\rangle+2\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+},{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{G}}_{0}^{2}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}\rangle+\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+},{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{G}}_{0}^{2}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}\rangle.

For the first term, we have directly from Proposition
B.8
that

\left|\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0},{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{G}}_{0}^{2}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}\rangle-p\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0})\right|\leq C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{3}(p)\cdot p\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0}).

(160)

For the two other terms, notice that they correspond to the terms (
101
) and (
102
) in Proposition
B.9
with
{\boldsymbol{v}}={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}
and
{\boldsymbol{u}}={\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}
, where

\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{f}_{0,j}\langle\boldsymbol{f}_{+,j},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}\rangle]=0,\qquad\quad\mathbb{E}[u_{i}^{2}]=\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\|_{2}^{2}.

Hence, by Proposition
B.9
, we have with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
that

\displaystyle\left|\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+},{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{G}}_{0}^{2}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}\rangle-\frac{1}{\nu_{2,0}^{2}}\frac{\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}\|_{2}^{2}}{p-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2,0})^{-2})}\right|\leq C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{4}(p)\cdot\frac{p\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}\|_{2}^{2}}{\gamma_{+}^{2}},

(161)

\displaystyle\left|\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+},{\boldsymbol{F}}_{+}^{\mathsf{T}}{\boldsymbol{G}}_{0}^{2}{\boldsymbol{F}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}\rangle\right|\leq C_{*,D,K}\cdot{\mathcal{E}}_{4}(p)\cdot\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}\|_{2}\frac{p^{2}\nu_{2,0}}{\gamma_{+}^{2}}\sqrt{\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0})}.

Furthermore, by Lemma
B.20
stated below

\left|\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2,0})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}\rangle+\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}\rangle/\nu_{2,0}^{2}}{p-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2,0})^{-2})}-p\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*})\right|\leq C\frac{\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)}{p}\cdot p\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*}).

(162)

Combining Eqs. (
154
), (
155
), (
156
), and (
157
), we deduce that
with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
,

\displaystyle\left|p\widetilde{\Phi}_{4}({\boldsymbol{F}};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*},p\nu_{1})-p\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*})\right|\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\cdot\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)^{2}{\mathcal{E}}_{4}(p)\cdot p\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*}).

(163)

where we used that

\displaystyle\frac{p\nu_{2,0}}{\gamma_{+}}\sqrt{\frac{p\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\|_{2}^{2}}{\gamma_{+}^{2}}p\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0})}\leq C\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)\left\{\frac{\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\|_{2}^{2}/\nu_{2,0}^{2}}{p-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2,0})^{-2})}+p\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2,0};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{0})\right\},

\displaystyle\frac{p\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}\|_{2}^{2}}{\gamma_{+}^{2}}\leq C\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)^{2}\frac{\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\|_{2}^{2}/\nu_{2,0}^{2}}{p-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2,0})^{-2})}.

Step 3: Combining the terms.

From Eqs. (
158
) and (
163
), we have with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
that

\displaystyle\left|(p\nu_{1})^{2}\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},{\boldsymbol{R}}^{2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle-\nu_{2}\Psi_{1}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*})+(p\nu_{1})p\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*})\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{*,D,K}\cdot\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)^{2}{\mathcal{E}}_{4}(p)\cdot\left[\nu_{2}\Psi_{1}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*})+(p\nu_{1})p\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*})\right].

First, it is straightforward to verify that indeed

\nu_{2}\Psi_{1}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*})-(p\nu_{1})p\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*})=\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{n,p}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},\lambda).

Then by Eq. (
121
) in Lemma
B.14
, we conclude that with probability at least
1-p^{-D}
,

\left|(p\nu_{1})^{2}\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},{\boldsymbol{R}}^{2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\rangle-\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{n,p}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},\lambda)\right|\leq C_{*,D,K}\cdot\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)^{2}{\mathcal{E}}_{4}(p)\cdot\widetilde{\mathsf{B}}_{n,p}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},\lambda),

which concludes the proof of this lemma.
∎

Lemma B.20
.

Assuming that
p^{2}\xi_{{\mathsf{m}}}^{2}\leq\gamma_{+}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\nu_{2,0}\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0},({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2,0})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}\rangle+\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}\|_{2}^{2}-\nu_{2}\Psi_{1}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*})\right|\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C}{p}\nu_{2}\Psi_{1}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*}),

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2,0})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}\rangle+\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{+}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{+}\rangle/\nu_{2,0}^{2}}{p-{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}^{2}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}+\nu_{2,0})^{-2})}-p\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*})\right|\leq

\displaystyle C\frac{\rho_{\gamma_{+}}(p)}{p}\cdot p\Psi_{3}(\nu_{2};{\boldsymbol{A}}_{*}).

Proof of Lemma
B.20
.

This lemma follows from the same arguments as in the proofs of Lemma
B.5
and Lemma
B.19
.
∎

Appendix C
Details of the numerical illustration

In this section we provide further examples of comparison between the excess risk computed using the deterministic equivalent in Theorem
3.3
and numerical simulations, together with details about their realization. Results from numerical experiments are obtained averaging over 20-50 seeds. The data dimension is
d=100
, with the exception of experiments involving real data (see Appendix
C.4
) and the ones realized considering the Gaussian design described in Appendix
C.2
.

C.1
Self-consistent equations

To solve Equations
18
and
19
numerically, the following approach has been employed. From equation
19
,

\sqrt{\left(1-\frac{n}{p}\right)^{2}+4\frac{\lambda}{p\nu_{2}}}=2\frac{\nu_{1}}{\nu_{2}}-1+\frac{n}{p}.

(164)

Substituting this expression in equation
18
, we obtain

\displaystyle 2\left(1-\frac{\nu_{1}}{\nu 2}\right)=\frac{2}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-1})

(165)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nu_{2}>0}}{{\implies}}

\displaystyle\nu_{2}=\nu_{1}+\frac{\nu_{2}}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2})^{-1}).

(166)

Therefore, the parameters
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
have been computed by iterating

\displaystyle\nu_{1}^{t+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\nu_{2}^{t}}{2}\left[1-\frac{n}{p}+\sqrt{\left(1-\frac{n}{p}\right)^{2}+4\frac{\lambda}{p\nu_{2}^{t}}}\right],

(167)

\displaystyle\nu_{2}^{t+1}

\displaystyle=\nu_{1}^{t+1}+\frac{\nu_{2}^{t}}{p}{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}+\nu_{2}^{t})^{-1}),

(168)

until a chosen tolerance
\epsilon
was reached.

C.2
Gaussian design

Figures
3
,
4
and
9
have been realized considering Gaussian design for the vectors in ’feature space’ defined in Appendix
B.1
. In particular, fixed
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}
, we have drawn

\{{\boldsymbol{g}}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}\sim_{\text{i.i.d.}}\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{I}),\quad\{\boldsymbol{f}_{j}\}_{j\in[p]}\sim_{\text{i.i.d.}}\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{0},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}),

and consequently
\{y_{i}={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{i}\}_{i\in[n]}
. Then the random feature estimator can be computed according to eqs. (
61
) and (
62
). In the figures produced with this setting, the elements of
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}
and
\operatorname{diag}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
follow power-laws truncated at the component
10^{4}
.

Figure 4:
Relative difference between the excess risk (
eq.

6
) of random features ridge regression from numerical simulation and its deterministic equivalent (
Theorem

3.3
), with regularization strength
\lambda=0.1
, and noise variance
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}=0.1
. The relative error is
O((n\wedge p)^{-1/2})
, in agreement with

32
. The simulations are made following the procedure described in
section

C.2
, with
\xi_{k}=k^{-1.2}
and

; (

)

fixed (

)
n=3000
fixed.

C.3
Empirical diagonalization

Whenever the data probability distribution
\mu_{x}
or the weights distribution
\mu_{w}
are unknown (e.g. in all cases involving real data), we estimated the matrix
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
and the vector
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}
following the procedure described in this section and summarized in Algorithm
1
.
Consider a data set of
N
covariates
\{{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\}_{i\in[N]}
drawn from
\mu_{x}
and
N
noiseless labels
\{y_{i}=f_{*}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})\}_{i\in[N]}
, and a set of
P
weights
\{{\boldsymbol{w}}_{j}\}_{j\in[P]}
. In Figures
1
,
5
,
6
,
7
,
8
, for which this procedure was used, we take both
N,P=10^{4}
(with the exception of Fig.
7
(right), where
P=8000
) and approximate
\mu_{x}
and
\mu_{w}
respectively with the empirical distributions
\tilde{\mu}_{x}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}N^{-1}\delta({\boldsymbol{x}}-{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})
and
\tilde{\mu}_{w}=\sum_{j=1}^{P}P^{-1}\delta({\boldsymbol{w}}-{\boldsymbol{w}}_{j})
. Then eq. (
2
) becomes

K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{w}}\sim\mu_{w}}\left[\varphi({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{w}})\varphi({\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{w}})\right]=\sum_{j=1}^{P}P^{-1}\varphi({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{j})\varphi({\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{j}),

(169)

and since eq. (
11
) implies
\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})\psi_{k}({\boldsymbol{x}})=\xi_{k}^{2}\psi_{k}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})
, defining the Gram matrix
\boldsymbol{K}^{\text{emp}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
with elements
\tilde{K}_{ii^{\prime}}=K({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i^{\prime}})N^{-1}
and the vectors
\boldsymbol{\tilde{\psi}}^{k}=(\psi_{k}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}),\ldots,\psi_{k}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{N}))^{\top}
, we can write the following eigenvalue problems, for
k\in[N]
:

\boldsymbol{\tilde{K}}\boldsymbol{\tilde{\psi}}_{k}=\tilde{\xi}_{k}^{2}\boldsymbol{\tilde{\psi}}_{k}.

(170)

We then constructed the matrix
\tilde{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}=\operatorname{diag}(\tilde{\xi}_{1}^{2},\ldots,\tilde{\xi}_{N}^{2})
and used it as an approximation of
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
. One should note that in this situation
\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\psi_{k}({\boldsymbol{x}})\psi_{k^{\prime}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=\delta_{kk^{\prime}}
corresponds to
\boldsymbol{\tilde{\psi}}_{k}\boldsymbol{\tilde{\psi}}_{k^{\prime}}=N\delta_{kk^{\prime}}
.

Similarly, eq. (
13
) implies
\beta_{*,k}=\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{x}}\left[f_{*}({\boldsymbol{x}})\psi_{k}({\boldsymbol{x}})\right]
, which can be approximated by

\tilde{\beta_{k}}=N^{-1}\boldsymbol{y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\tilde{\psi}}_{k}.

(171)

Algorithm 1

Empirical diagonalization

\{{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\}_{i\in[N]}\sim_{\text{i.i.d.}}\mu_{x}
,

\{y_{i}=f_{*}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})\}_{i\in[N]}
,
\{{\boldsymbol{w}}_{j}\}_{j\in[P]}\sim_{\text{i.i.d.}}\mu_{w}

{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}
,
\mathsf{R}_{n,p}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},\lambda)

for

i,i^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,N\}

do

\tilde{K}_{ii^{\prime}}\leftarrow(NP)^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{P}\varphi({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{j})\varphi({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i^{\prime}},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{j})

end

for

\{(\tilde{\xi}_{k},\boldsymbol{\tilde{\psi}}_{k})_{k\in[N]}\}\leftarrow\operatorname{eig}(\boldsymbol{\tilde{K}})

\triangleright

\boldsymbol{\tilde{\psi}}_{k}\boldsymbol{\tilde{\psi}}_{k^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}N\delta_{kk^{\prime}}

for

k\in\{1,\ldots,N\}

do

\tilde{\beta}_{k}\leftarrow N^{-1}{\boldsymbol{y}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\tilde{\psi}}_{k}

end

for

{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\leftarrow\operatorname{diag}(\tilde{\xi}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\xi}_{N})

{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*}\leftarrow(\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{N})^{\top}

Iterate eqs. (
167
-
168
) up to tolerance
\epsilon

Compute the deterministic equivalent for the excess risk (
22
-
24
)

C.4
Real data

We performed numerical simulations sampling the training data from the MNIST data set
Lecun et al. [1998]
and the FashionMNIST data set
Xiao et al. [2017]
, standardizing both covariates and labels, reshaping the images into vectors with
d=748
. Results are shown in Figures
1
(right) and
7
(left).

C.5
Trained network

In Figure
7
(right), we apply the procedure described in Appendix
C.3
to the trained weights of a two layer neural network with hidden layer of size
p

\hat{f}({\boldsymbol{x}};{\boldsymbol{W}},{\boldsymbol{a}})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\sum_{j=1}^{p}a_{j}\varphi(\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{j}\rangle).

At initialization, the weights are randomly drawn; then, after sampling a training dataset
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\text{tr}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{\text{tr}}\times d}
,
{\boldsymbol{y}}_{\text{tr}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{\text{tr}}}
, the weights of the first layer are trained using gradient descent, iterating for
t=1,\ldots,T
the following

{\boldsymbol{W}}_{t+1}={\boldsymbol{W}}_{t}+\frac{\eta}{n_{\text{tr}}}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\text{tr}}^{\top}\left[\left(({\boldsymbol{y}}_{\text{tr}}-\hat{f}(\langle{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\text{tr}};{\boldsymbol{W}}_{t},{\boldsymbol{a}}))^{\top}{\boldsymbol{a}}\right)\odot\varphi^{\prime}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{\text{tr}}{\boldsymbol{W}}_{t}^{\top})\right],

(172)

where
\eta
is the learning rate,
\odot
is the Hadamard product,
\hat{f}
and
\varphi
are applied component-wise; finally, the weights of the second layer are minimized using ridge regression, as in eq. (
5
).

Appendix D
Derivation of the rates

In this appendix we present a sketch of the derivation of the decay rates for the excess error given in Section
4
. We choose

\displaystyle p=n^{q},\quad\lambda=n^{-(\ell-1)},\quad\text{ with }q,l\geq 0

and we assume

\eta_{k}=k^{-\alpha},\quad\beta_{*k}=k^{-\frac{1+2\alpha r}{2}},\quad\text{ with }\alpha>1,\;r>0,

which follows the source and capacity conditions stated in (
37
).
In order to simplify the derivation of the rates, we introduce the following notation:

\displaystyle T^{s}_{\delta\gamma}(\nu)\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{k^{-s-\delta\alpha}}{(k^{-\alpha}+\nu)^{\gamma}},

\displaystyle s\in{0,1},\;0\leq\delta\leq\gamma.

For
s+\alpha(\delta-\gamma)<1
and in the limit
\nu\to 0
, this term can be written as a Riemann sum as follows

\displaystyle T^{s}_{\delta\gamma}(\nu)

\displaystyle=\nu^{-\gamma+\delta+\nicefrac{{s}}{{\alpha}}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{(k\nu^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{\alpha}}})^{-s-\delta\alpha}}{((k\nu^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{\alpha}}})^{-\alpha}+1)^{\gamma}}

(173)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nu\to 0}}{{\approx}}\nu^{-(\gamma-\delta)-\nicefrac{{(1-s)}}{{\alpha}}}\int_{\nu^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{\alpha}}}}^{\infty}\frac{x^{-s-\delta\alpha}}{(x^{-\alpha}+1)^{\gamma}}=O\left(\nu^{-(\gamma-\delta)-\nicefrac{{(1-s)}}{{\alpha}}}\right).

(174)

Otherwise, if
s+\alpha(\delta-\gamma)>1
, we can write:

\displaystyle T^{s}_{\delta\gamma}(\nu)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\left\lfloor\nu^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{\alpha}}}\right\rfloor}\frac{k^{-s-\delta\alpha}}{(k^{-\alpha}+\nu)^{\gamma}}+\sum_{k=\left\lfloor\nu^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{\alpha}}}\right\rfloor+1}^{\infty}\frac{k^{-s-\delta\alpha}}{(k^{-\alpha}+\nu)^{\gamma}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nu\to 0}}{{\approx}}O(1)+\nu^{-(\gamma-\delta)-\nicefrac{{(1-s)}}{{\alpha}}}\int_{1+\nu^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{\alpha}}}}^{\infty}\frac{x^{-s-\delta\alpha}}{(x^{-\alpha}+1)^{\gamma}}=O(1).

Hence, for
\nu\to 0
,

T_{\delta\gamma}^{s}(\nu)=O\left(\nu^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{\alpha}}\left[s-1+\alpha(\delta-\gamma)\right]\wedge 0}\right).

(175)

Rewriting (
18
-
19
) as follows, we study the dependence of the positive parameters
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
with
n
:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\nu_{2}=\frac{\nu_{2}}{p}T^{0}_{11}(\nu_{2})+\nu_{1}\\
\nu_{1}=\frac{\nu_{1}}{n}T^{0}_{11}(\nu_{2})+\frac{\lambda}{n}\end{cases}

.
In the limit
n\to\infty
, we can distinguish the following regimes

\displaystyle\begin{cases}T_{11}^{0}(\nu_{2})\ll n,p\\
\nu_{1}=n^{-1}\lambda\left(1+O\left(T_{11}^{0}(\nu_{2})n^{-1}\right)\right)\\
\nu_{2}=n^{-1}\lambda\left(1+O\left(T_{11}^{0}(\nu_{2})(n\wedge p)^{-1}\right)\right)\end{cases}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nu_{2}=o(1)}}{{\implies}}\begin{cases}\nu_{2}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\alpha}}}\ll n^{1\wedge q}\implies\ell<\alpha(1\wedge q)\\
\nu_{1}\approx\nu_{2}\approx n^{-\ell}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}T_{11}^{0}(\nu_{2})\ll p\\
\nu_{1}\gg\lambda n^{-1}\\
T_{11}^{0}(\nu_{2})=n-\left(n\nu_{1}\right)^{-1}\lambda\\
\nu_{2}=\nu_{1}(1+O\left(T_{11}^{0}(\nu_{2})p^{-1}\right))\end{cases}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\implies}}\begin{cases}\nu_{2}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\alpha}}}\ll n^{q}\\
\nu_{1}\gg n^{-\ell}\\
\nu_{2}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\alpha}}}=O\left(n+o(n)\right)\\
\nu_{2}=\nu_{1}\left(1+o(1)\right)\end{cases}\implies\begin{cases}q>1,\;\ell>\alpha\\
\nu_{1}\approx\nu_{2}\propto n^{-\alpha}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}T_{11}^{0}(\nu_{2})\ll n\\
\nu_{1}\ll\nu_{2}\\
\nu_{1}=n^{-1}\lambda\left(1+O\left(T_{11}^{0}(\nu_{2})n^{-1}\right)\right)\\
T_{11}^{0}(\nu_{2})=p-\nu_{1}\nu_{2}^{-1}\end{cases}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\implies}}\begin{cases}\nu_{2}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\alpha}}}\ll n\\
\nu_{1}\ll\nu_{2}\\
\nu_{1}=n^{-\ell}(1+o(1))\\
\nu_{2}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\alpha}}}=n^{q}-o(1)\end{cases}\implies\begin{cases}q<\left(1\wedge\ell\alpha^{-1}\right)\\
\nu_{1}\approx n^{-\ell}\\
\nu_{2}\propto n^{-\alpha q}\end{cases}

In
(a)
we have used the fact that, for
\nu_{2}
constant or diverging with
n
,
T_{11}^{0}(\nu_{2})
is respctively
O(1)
or infinitesimal, while we have that
T_{11}^{0}(\nu_{2})
is diverging in both cases. Hence,
\nu_{2}
must be infinitesimal, allowing us to use (
173
).

In conclusion, as
n\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\nu_{1}

\displaystyle\approx\begin{cases}O\left(n^{-\alpha}\right),\quad\text{for }q>1\text{ and }\ell>\alpha,\\
n^{-\ell},\quad\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(176)

\displaystyle\nu_{2}

\displaystyle\approx O\left(n^{-\alpha\left(1\wedge q\wedge\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{\alpha}}\right)}\right),

(177)

in particular

\frac{\nu_{1}}{\nu_{2}}=\begin{cases}1-O\left(\nu_{2}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\alpha}}}n^{-q}\right),\quad\text{for }q>1\wedge\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{\alpha}}\\
O\left(n^{-\ell+\alpha q}\right)=o(1)\quad\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

(178)

D.1
Variance term

Considering the results (
176
-
178
), we can write eq. (
20
) as

\displaystyle\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{p}{n}\left[\left(1-\frac{\nu_{1}}{\nu_{2}}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{\nu_{1}}{\nu_{2}}\right)^{2}\frac{T_{22}^{0}(\nu_{2})}{p-T_{22}^{0}(\nu_{2})}\right]

(179)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}n^{-1}O(\nu_{2}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\alpha}}})=O\left(n^{-(1-(1\wedge\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{\alpha}}))}\right),\quad\text{for }q>1\wedge\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{\alpha}}\\
n^{-(1-q)}(1+o(1))\quad\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(180)

One could notice, using the integral approximation of the Riemann sum
T_{22}^{0}(\nu_{2})
given in (
173
), that
1-\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})=O(1)
for any choice of
\ell
and
q
. Hence, the variance term given by (
23
) decays with
n
with rate

\gamma_{\mathcal{V}}(\ell,q)=1-\left(\frac{\ell}{\alpha}\wedge q\wedge 1\right).

D.2
Bias term

Using again (
176
-
178
), we can compute the rate of
\chi(\nu_{2})
defined in eq. (
21
), as
n\to\infty
:

\displaystyle\chi(\nu_{2})=\frac{T_{12}^{0}(\nu_{2})}{p-T_{22}^{0}(\nu_{2}))}

\displaystyle=n^{-q}O\left(\nu_{2}^{-1-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\alpha}}}\right)\left(1+n^{-q}O\left(\nu_{2}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\alpha}}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=n^{-q}O\left(\nu_{2}^{-1-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\alpha}}}\right)

Using the integral approximation given in (
173
), one could verify that
p-T_{22}^{0}(\nu_{2})=O(p)
for any choice of
\ell
and
q
.

The deterministic equivalent for the bias term, given in eq. (
22
), can be written as

\displaystyle\mathsf{B}_{n,p}({\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{*},\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{\nu_{2}^{2}}{1-\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})}\left(T_{2r,2}^{1}(\nu_{2})+\chi(\nu_{2})T_{2r+1,2}^{1}(\nu_{2})\right)

(181)

\displaystyle=O\left(\nu_{2}^{2}\right)O\left(\nu_{2}^{2(r-1\wedge 0)}+n^{-q}\nu_{2}^{-1-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\alpha}}+(2r-1)\wedge 0}\right)

(182)

\displaystyle=O\left(\nu_{2}^{2(r\wedge 1)}+n^{-q}\nu_{2}^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{\alpha}}+2(r\wedge\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})}\right)

(183)

where we have used (
175
) to compute the scalings of the terms
T^{1}{\delta\gamma}
and the fact that
1-\Upsilon(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})=O(1)
.

From eq. (
183
), and using the result (
177
), it is straightforward to see that the decay rate of the bias term is given by

\gamma_{\mathcal{B}}=\left[2\alpha\left(\frac{\ell}{\alpha}\wedge q\wedge 1\right)(r\wedge 1)\right]\wedge\left[\left(2\alpha\left(r\wedge\frac{1}{2}\right)-1\right)\left(\frac{\ell}{\alpha}\wedge q\wedge 1\right)+q\right].

(184)

Examples of the results of Theorem
4.1
and Corollary
4.2
are shown in Fig.

and
9
.

D.3
Details of Remark
4.1

In order to extend the results of Theorem
4.1
and Corollary
4.2
to the excess risk defined in (
6
), in this section we compute the intervals for
\ell
and
q
such that the assumptions of
Theorem

3.3
hold and the approximation rates
{\mathcal{E}}(n,p)
are vanishing, under source and capacity conditions.

Given
n
,
p=n^{q}
,
\lambda^{-(\ell-1)}
and
\nu_{2}
as in (
177
) assumption
3.2
is verified for
{\rm m}=n^{q+\ell}
and
{\sf C}_{*}=O(\nu_{2}^{-1})
. In fact

\displaystyle\sum_{k={\rm m}+1}^{\infty}\xi^{2}_{k}>\int_{\rm m+1}^{\infty}x^{-\alpha}=\frac{({\rm m}+1)^{1-\alpha}}{\alpha-1}

(185)

and the inequality in (
16
) holds if

\displaystyle n^{2q}({\rm m}+1)^{-\alpha}\leq n^{q-\ell}\frac{({\rm m+1})^{1-\alpha}}{\alpha-1}\implies{\rm m}\geq n^{q+\ell}(\alpha-1)-1.

(186)

The inequalities in (
17
) can be written as

\displaystyle{\sf C}_{*}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{T^{0}_{11}(\nu_{2})}{T^{0}_{22}(\nu_{2})}=O(1),

(187)

\displaystyle{\sf C}_{*}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{T^{1}_{2r,1}(\nu_{2})}{\nu_{2}T^{1}_{2r,2}(\nu_{2})}=O\left(\nu_{2}^{-((0\vee(2r-1))\wedge 1)}\right).

(188)

Then, introducing
\eta_{*}\in(0,\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})
, we can compute the following quantities of interest (introduced in
eqs.

25
,
26
and
27
):

\displaystyle r_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}(\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot k\rfloor)

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\geq\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot k\rfloor})}{||{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\geq\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot k\rfloor}||_{\rm op}}=O(\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot k\rfloor)=O(k)

(189)

\displaystyle M_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}(k)

\displaystyle\coloneqq 1+\frac{r_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}(\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot k\rfloor)\vee k}{k}\log\left(r_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}(\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot k\rfloor)\vee k\right)=1+O(\log k),

(190)

\displaystyle\rho_{\kappa}(p)

\displaystyle\coloneqq 1+\frac{p\cdot\xi^{2}_{\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot p\rfloor}}{\kappa}M_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}(p)=1+O\left(\frac{n^{q(1-\alpha)}}{\kappa}\log n\right),

(191)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}_{\kappa}(n,p)

\displaystyle\coloneqq 1+\mathbbm{1}[n\leq p/\eta_{*}]\cdot\left\{\frac{n\xi_{\lfloor\eta_{*}\cdot n\rfloor}^{2}}{\kappa}+\frac{n}{p}\cdot\rho_{\kappa}(p)\right\}M_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}(n)

(192)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\geq\eta_{*}^{1/(1-q)}}}{{=}}1+\mathbbm{1}[q\geq 1]O\left(\frac{n^{1-\alpha}}{\kappa}\log n\right).

(193)

Similarly, considering
\nu_{1}
scaling as
in
eq.

176
, we have that
eq.

31

\displaystyle\gamma_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\frac{p\lambda}{n}+\sum_{k={\mathsf{m}}+1}^{\infty}\xi_{k}^{2}=O\left(n^{q-\ell}+n^{(q+\ell)(1-\alpha)}\right),

(194)

\displaystyle\gamma_{+}

\displaystyle=p\nu_{1}+\sum_{k={\mathsf{m}}+1}^{\infty}\xi_{k}^{2}=O\left(\mathbbm{1}[q\geq 1]n^{q-(\ell\wedge\alpha)}+\mathbbm{1}[q<1]n^{q-\ell}+n^{(q+\ell)(1-\alpha)}\right)

(195)

\displaystyle=O\left(\mathbbm{1}[q\geq 1]n^{q-(\ell\wedge\alpha)}+\mathbbm{1}\left[\frac{\ell}{\alpha}(2-\alpha)\leq q<1\right]n^{q-\ell}+\mathbbm{1}\left[q<\frac{\ell}{\alpha}(2-\alpha)\right]n^{(q+\ell)(1-\alpha)}\right).

(196)

The last step is a consequence of

\displaystyle q-(\ell\wedge\alpha)>(q+\ell)(1-\alpha)

\displaystyle\implies q>\frac{\ell}{\alpha}\underbrace{(1-\alpha)}_{<0}+\left(\frac{\ell}{\alpha}\wedge 1\right),

(197)

\displaystyle q-\ell>(q+\ell)(1-\alpha)

\displaystyle\implies q>\frac{\ell}{\alpha}(2-\alpha)

(198)

Fixing
K>0
and considering the , we consider condition (
28
):

\displaystyle\lambda\geq n^{-K}

\displaystyle\impliedby\ell\leq 1+K,

(199)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\lambda}\geq p^{-K}

\displaystyle\impliedby\begin{cases}\ell\leq q(1+K)\\
q>\frac{(2-a)}{a}\ell\end{cases}\vee\begin{cases}\ell\leq\frac{q(1+K-\alpha)}{\alpha-1}\\
q<\frac{(2-a)}{a}\ell\end{cases},

(200)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}_{\lambda}(n,p)^{5/2}\cdot\log^{3/2}(n)\leq~K\sqrt{n}

\displaystyle\impliedby\ell>\mathbbm{1}[q\geq 1]\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{5}\right),

(201)

and, similarly, condition (
29
) is satisfied if, for
q\geq 1

\displaystyle\left(1+O\left(n^{\ell-\alpha}\log n\right)\right)^{2}\left(O\left(1+n^{(\ell\wedge\alpha)-q\alpha}\log n\right)\right)^{8}q\log^{4}n\leq Kn^{\nicefrac{{q}}{{2}}}

(202)

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle 2(\ell-\alpha)\vee 0<\frac{q}{2}\implies\ell<\frac{q}{4}+\alpha,

(203)

while, for
q<1
, if

\displaystyle\begin{cases}1>q\geq\frac{\ell}{\alpha}(2-\alpha)\\
\left(1+O\left(n^{\ell-q\alpha}\right)\right)^{8}q\log^{4}n\leq Kn^{\nicefrac{{q}}{{2}}}\end{cases}\vee\begin{cases}q<\frac{\ell}{\alpha}(2-\alpha)\\
\left(1+O\left(n^{\ell(\alpha-1)}\right)\right)^{8}q\log^{4}n\leq Kn^{\nicefrac{{q}}{{2}}}\end{cases}

(204)

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\begin{cases}1>q\geq\frac{\ell}{\alpha}(2-\alpha)\\
\ell<\alpha q+\frac{q}{16}\end{cases}\vee\begin{cases}q<\frac{\ell}{\alpha}(2-\alpha)\\
\ell<\frac{q}{16(\alpha-1)}\end{cases}

(205)

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\ell<q\left(\left(\alpha+\frac{1}{16}\right)\vee\frac{1}{16(\alpha-1)}\right),\text{}

(206)

where the last step is a consequence of

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha}{2-\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha+\frac{1}{16}\leq\frac{1}{16(\alpha-1)},

\displaystyle\text{ for }\alpha\geq\overline{\alpha}\coloneqq\frac{15+\sqrt{353}}{32}\approx 1.05588,

(207)

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha}{2-\alpha}

\displaystyle>\alpha+\frac{1}{16}>\frac{1}{16(\alpha-1)},

\displaystyle\text{ for }\alpha<\overline{\alpha}.

(208)

Finally, the approximation rate defined in
remark

4.1
is

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}(n,p)=

\displaystyle\left(n^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}+\mathbbm{1}[q\geq 1]\tilde{O}\left(n^{6(\ell-\alpha)-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}\right)\right)+

(209)

\displaystyle\left(n^{-\nicefrac{{q}}{{2}}}+\mathbbm{1}[q\geq 1]\tilde{O}\left(n^{2(\ell-\alpha)-\nicefrac{{q}}{{2}}}\right)\right)\left(1+\tilde{O}\left(\frac{n^{8q(1-\alpha)}}{\gamma_{+}^{8}}\right)\right),

(210)

where the second term vanishes under the conditions in (
203
) and (
206
), and the first term vanishes by further assuming, for
q\geq 1

\ell<\alpha+\frac{1}{12}.

(211)

Appendix E
Comparison with neural scaling laws

In this appendix we discuss the relationship between our results and the recent literature of the theory of neural scaling laws with linear models. We adopt a notation close to ours, with dictionary to their notation given in
Table

1
and
Table

2
.

Bahri et al. [2024]
and
Maloney et al. [2022]
have considered a model where with Gaussian input data and linear target function:

\displaystyle f_{\star}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})=\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\star},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\rangle,

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}),

\displaystyle i\in[n]

(212)

The covariance matrix
{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}={\rm diag}(\lambda_{k})_{k\in[d]}
is taken to be diagonal, with eigenvalues following a power-law scaling:

\displaystyle\lambda_{k}\sim\left(\frac{d}{k}\right)^{\alpha},

\displaystyle k\in[d]

(213)

with
\alpha>1
and the target weights are assumed to be random Gaussian vectors
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\star}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\nicefrac{{1}}{{d}}I_{d})
. In particular, note that
{\rm Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}\sim d
for
d\to\infty
. Given the training data, they consider least-squares regression in the class of linear random features predictor:

\displaystyle\hat{f}({\boldsymbol{x}};{\boldsymbol{a}})=\langle{\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{W}}{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle

(214)

where
{\boldsymbol{W}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times d}
is a Gaussian random matrix elements in
\mathcal{N}(0,\nicefrac{{1}}{{d}}I_{d})
.
5
5

5

In
[
Maloney et al., 2022
]
,
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\star}
has variance
\nicefrac{{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{w}}}{{d}}
, the spectrum has a scale
\lambda_{-}
and the random projection
{\boldsymbol{W}}
has variance
\nicefrac{{\sigma_{u}}}{{d}}
. Here we take
\sigma_{w}=\lambda_{-}=\sigma_{u}=1
since it is irrelevant to the discussion.

Table 2:
Dictionary of notation between the source and capacity conditions defined in
eq.

38
and the scalings in different neural scaling laws works. Note that since
Paquette et al. [2024]
also employs the greek letter “
\alpha
”, we denote theirs by
\tilde{\alpha}
to avoid confusion.

This setting is a particular case of the one introduced in
Section

2
. In particular, it satisfies particular source and capacity conditions
eq.

38
. To see this, note that the feature population covariance is identical to the input data covariance:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{W}}{\boldsymbol{x}}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{W}}^{\top}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{d}}{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}

(215)

Therefore, we can identify
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}=\nicefrac{{1}}{{d}}{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
which has
{\rm Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}<\infty
for
\alpha>1
in the limit
d\to\infty
. Therefore, the features satisfy a capacity condition with scaling
\alpha
. Moreover, the asymptotic kernel is simply the linear kernel:

\displaystyle K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{w}}}[\langle{\boldsymbol{w}},{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle\langle{\boldsymbol{w}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\rangle]=\frac{\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\prime}\rangle}{d}.

(216)

Since the target variance is constant, this is equivalent to a source condition with:

\displaystyle r=\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)

(217)

Since
\alpha\in(1,\infty)
, we are always in the hard regime
r\in(0,\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})
where the target does not belong to the RKHS
\mathcal{H}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Indeed, since
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\star}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\nicefrac{{1}}{{d}}I_{d})
, we have:

\displaystyle||f_{\star}||^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{d}\beta_{\star,k}^{2}\lambda_{k}\sim d^{\alpha-1}

(218)

which indeed diverges as
d\to\infty
. Moreover, note that least-squares regression correspond to the case
\ell=\infty
.

From the discussion above, the bias term scalings from
Bahri et al. [2024]
(resolution limited regime) and
Maloney et al. [2022]
(underparametrized regime
n\gg p
,
i.e.

q\ll 1
, and overparametrized regime
n\ll p
,
i.e.

q\gg 1
), correspond to a vertical cross-section on the large
\ell
region of Fig.
2
(Right). Indeed, we recover exactly the rate of the
label term
in eqs. (167)-(168) of
Maloney et al. [2022]
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(f_{\star},{\boldsymbol{X}},{\boldsymbol{W}},{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},\lambda)=O\left(n^{-2\alpha rq}\right)=O\left(p^{-(\alpha-1)}\right),

\displaystyle n\gg p,

(219)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(f_{\star},{\boldsymbol{X}},{\boldsymbol{W}},{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},\lambda)=O\left(n^{-2\alpha r}\right)=O\left(n^{-(\alpha-1)}\right),

\displaystyle n\ll p.

(220)

Similarly, it is possible to recover the rates for the
noise term
(first two results in eq. (86) of
Maloney et al. [2022]
) as the vertical cross-section on the large
\ell
region of Figure
2
for the rates of the variance term. In particular:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}(f_{\star},{\boldsymbol{X}},{\boldsymbol{W}},{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},\lambda)=\begin{cases}O\left(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}n^{-(1-q)}\right)=O\left(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\frac{p}{n}\right),\quad n\gg p\\
O\left(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}n^{0}\right),\quad p\gg n\end{cases}.

(221)

Finally, contemporarily to the publication of this work,
Atanasov et al. [2024]
has extended the discussion of the linear random features model in
eq.

212
to the case where the target weights
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
decay as a power-law, allowing to change the source and capacity coefficients independently. Following a private discussion with the authors, it was realized that the rates derived in
Section

4
can be put in a one-to-one correspondence with the ones derived from
Atanasov et al. [2024]
. We refer the interested reader to
Table

1
for a dictionary between the notation of these works, and Section VI.6 of
Atanasov et al. [2024]
for a detailed discussion of this relationship. We stress, however, that beside being rigorous, our results hold for features in infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces and are not restricted to a particular asymptotic limit in the dimensions.

Comparison with the SGD rates from
Paquette et al. [2024]
,
Lin et al. [2024]
—

Furthermore, in the linear noiseless target setting, lifting the condition in eq. (
217
), it is possible to compare our results to the compute-optimal rates for the risk obtained through stochastic gradient descent in
Paquette et al. [2024]
. In particular, we consider unitary batch-size and the correspondence between the number of iterations of stochastic gradient descent and the number of samples
n
in ridge regression. Then, defining
\hat{\gamma}
the decay rate of the compute-optimal curves for the risk
\hat{\mathcal{R}}\asymp n^{-\hat{\gamma}}
in
Paquette et al. [2024]
, corresponding to the compute-optimal number of features
p\asymp\hat{p}=:n^{\hat{q}}
,
6
6

6

Note that this quantity is denoted
d_{\star}
in
Paquette et al. [2024]
.
coincides with
\gamma_{\mathcal{B}}(\ell=1,\hat{q})
in Theorem
4.1
,
i.e.
with fixed regularization parameter
\lambda=1
(
\ell=1
). In particular, consider the following regions in the phase diagram provided in their work:
7
7

7

The Phases Ib, Ic and IV correspond to
\alpha<1
,
i.e.
to an activation
\sigma\notin L_{2}
.

•

Phase Ia (
r<\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
):

\displaystyle\hat{q}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha},

(222)

\displaystyle\hat{\gamma}

\displaystyle=2r=2\alpha\hat{q}r=\gamma_{\mathcal{B}}(1,\hat{q});

(223)

•

Phase II (
r>\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
and
r<1-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2\alpha}}<1
):

\displaystyle\hat{q}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+2\alpha r-\alpha}{\alpha}<1,

(224)

\displaystyle\hat{\gamma}

\displaystyle=2r=\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha}+\hat{q}=\gamma_{\mathcal{B}}(1,\hat{q});

(225)

•

Phase III (
r>\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
and
r>1-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2\alpha}}
):

\displaystyle\hat{q}

\displaystyle=1,

(226)

\displaystyle\hat{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\alpha-1}{\alpha}=\frac{\alpha-1}{\alpha}+\hat{q}=\gamma_{\mathcal{B}}(1,\hat{q}).

(227)

We emphasize that, in Phases Ia and II,
\hat{\gamma}=\max_{q}\gamma_{\mathcal{B}}(1,q)
, while, in Phase III,
\hat{\gamma}\leq\max_{q}\gamma_{\mathcal{B}}(1,q)
. Hence, the compute-optimal decay rate of the risk for stochastic gradient descent is equal or smaller than the largest rate achievable by RFRR with fixed regularization
\lambda=1
and therefore always smaller than the optimal one in Corollary
4.2
.

A similar setting has been investigated by the recent work
Lin et al. [2024]
, providing scaling laws for the excess risk obtained by stochastic gradient descent with stepsize schedule
\eta_{t}=\eta/2^{\nicefrac{{t\log n}}{{n}}}
, for
t=1,...,n
.
8
8

8

The stepsize in
Lin et al. [2024]
is denoted by the greek letter
\gamma
, which we changed to
\eta
to avoid confusion with the symbol we use for the risk decay rate.
Under the same source and capacity conditions, assuming
r\in(0,\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})
and
\eta=O(1)
, the result in their Theorem 4.2 may be rephrased as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{X}},{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}}\mathcal{R}(f_{\star},{\boldsymbol{X}},{\boldsymbol{W}},{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}},\eta)\asymp n^{-\gamma_{\rm SGD}(\eta,p)},

(228)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\rm SGD}(\eta,p)=\left[2\alpha r\left(\frac{1+\log_{n}\eta}{\alpha}\wedge\log_{n}p\right)\right]\wedge\left[1-\left(\frac{1+\log_{n}\eta}{\alpha}\wedge\log_{n}p\right)\right].

(229)

Hence, choosing
p\asymp n^{q}
and
\eta\asymp n^{\ell-1}
,
i.e.

\eta\asymp\lambda^{-1}
, provided
\eta=O(1)\implies\ell<1
,
this result recovers precisely the same rates as in our Theorem
4.1
.
